count: 25109)\\\\nTotal output lines: 935\\\\n\\\\n\\\\n===== methods =====\\\\n# Overview\\\\n\\\\nTo dissociate reward from other sensory stimuli and observe how hippocampal activity related to rewards evolves with experience, we performed two-photon (2P) calcium imaging of CA1 neurons expressing GCaMP7f in head-fixed mice learning a virtual reality (VR) navigation task (Fig. 1a\\u2013c). VR provided tight control over sensory stimuli and the animal\\u2019s trajectory, allowing us to observe neuronal remapping in relation to distant rewards, a phenomenon potentially less visible in freely moving scenarios. Furthermore, the task included multiple updates to a hidden reward zone across two environments, allowing us to dissociate spatially driven and reward-driven remapping. Imaging began on day\\\\u20091 of task acquisition, in which animals traversed a unidirectional 450\\\\u2009cm virtual linear track (for example, Environment 1 (ENV 1)) with a hidden 50\\\\u2009cm reward zone where sucrose water was delivered operantly for licking (Fig. 1d,e). On day\\\\u20093, the reward zone was moved within a \\u2018switch\\u2019 session to a new track location, signaled by automatic reward delivery on the first ten post-switch trials only if the mouse failed to lick in the new zone. On day\\\\u20095, the reward zone was moved to a third location, then returned to its original location on day\\\\u20097. On day\\\\u20098, the reward switch coincided with the introduction of a novel environment (for example, ENV 2), in which the switch order was then reversed (Fig. 1e). On \\u2018stay\\u2019 days, the last reward location from the previous day was maintained. A separate \\u2018fixed-condition\\u2019 group of mice (n\\\\u2009=\\\\u20093) experienced only one reward location in a single environment (ENV 1) (Extended Data Fig. 1), to control for the effect of experience. To maintain engagement, the reward was randomly omitted on ~15% of trials. At the end of the track, mice passed through a variable length gray \\u2018teleport\\u2019 zone (~50\\\\u2009cm\\\\u2009+\\\\u2009temporal jitter up to 10\\\\u2009s; Methods) before starting the next lap.\\\\n\\\\n# Behavioral training and VR tasks\\\\n\\\\n## Pre-training\\\\n\\\\nMice were pre-trained on a \\u2018running\\u2019 task on a 350\\\\u2009cm virtual linear track with random black and white checkerboard walls and a white floor to collect a cued reward. The reward location was marked by two large gray towers, initially positioned 50\\\\u2009cm down the track. The mouse was rewarded for licking within 25\\\\u2009cm of the towers, otherwise, an automatic reward was given at the towers. The towers then disappeared and quickly reappeared further down the track. Covering the distance to the current reward in under 20\\\\u2009s increased the inter-reward distance by 10\\\\u2009cm, but if the mouse took longer than 30\\\\u2009s, the distance decreased by 10\\\\u2009cm, with a maximum reward location of 300\\\\u2009cm. Upon consistent completion of 300\\\\u2009cm distance in under 20\\\\u2009s, the automatic reward was turned off, requiring the mice to lick to receive reward. At the end of the track, the mice passed through a black curtain and into a gray \\u2018teleport zone\\u2019 (50\\\\u2009cm long) that was equiluminant with the VR environment before re-entering the track from the beginning. Once mice were reliably licking and completing 200 laps of the pre-training track within a 30\\u201340\\\\u2009min period, they were advanced to the main task below and imaging (mean\\\\u2009\\u00b1\\\\u2009s.d., 5.8\\\\u2009\\u00b1\\\\u20091.9\\\\u2009days of running pre-training across 14 mice).\\\\n\\\\n## Hidden reward zone task\\\\n\\\\nThe main \\u2018switch\\u2019 task involved two virtual environments similar to those previously used to study hippocampal remapping, each with visually distinct features from the pre-training environment. Both environments consisted of a 450\\\\u2009cm linear track, with two colored towers (green in ENV 1, blue in ENV 2) and two patterned towers along the walls. Environments were further distinguished by the frequency of diagonal wall gratings (low, ENV 1; high, ENV 2) and color of the sky (dark gray, ENV 1; light gray, ENV 2). The reward zone was a \\u2018hidden\\u2019, unmarked 50\\\\u2009cm span at one of three possible locations along the track, each equidistantly spaced between the towers to control for proximity of each reward to visual landmarks: zone A, 80\\u2013130\\\\u2009cm; zone B, 200\\u2013250\\\\u2009cm; zone C, 320\\u2013370\\\\u2009cm. Only one reward zone was ever active at a time. Reward was randomly omitted on approximately 15% of trials, determined by a random number generator for each trial. Each lap terminated in a black curtain following by a \\u2018teleport period\\u2019 that began with an unchanging gray display for a randomly jittered amount of time (\\u2018jitter period\\u2019) between 1 and 5\\\\u2009s (5\\u201310\\\\u2009s on trials following reward omissions or trials in which the mouse did not lick in the reward zone), after which the gray display began to move again for 50\\\\u2009cm giving the appearance of a gray \\u2018tunnel,\\u2019 before the mouse re-entered the virtual environment at 0\\\\u2009cm.\\\\n\\\\nEach mouse encountered a different starting reward zone and sequence of reward zone switches, counterbalanced across mice (n\\\\u2009=\\\\u200911 mice). Most mice began the task in ENV 1 (n\\\\u2009=\\\\u20099 mice; two mice (m17 and m18) began in ENV 2). An initial reward zone (for example, A as in Fig. 1e) was acquired for days\\\\u20091\\u20132 of the task. On day\\\\u20093 (switch one), the zone was moved after 30 trials to one of the two other possible locations on the track (for example, B). On the first ten trials of any new condition, including the first day and each subsequent switch, the reward was automatically delivered at the end of the zone if the mouse had not yet licked within the zone, to signal reward availability. Otherwise, the reward was delivered at the first location within the zone where the mouse licked. After these ten trials, a reward was operantly delivered for licking within the zone, and we observed that the mice often started licking before ten trials elapsed (Extended Data Fig. 1a\\u2013c). The new reward zone was maintained on day\\\\u20094. On day\\\\u20095 (switch two), the zone was moved to the third possible reward zone (for example, C), maintained on day\\\\u20096 and moved back to the original location on day\\\\u20097 (for example, A). On day\\\\u20098, the reward zone switch coincided with a switch into the novel environment, where the sequence of zone switches was then reversed on the same day-to-day schedule for a total of 14\\\\u2009days (Fig. 1 and Extended Data Fig. 1). Each switch occurred after 30 trials.\\\\n\\\\nAn additional \\u2018fixed-condition\\u2019 cohort (n\\\\u2009=\\\\u20093 mice) experienced only ENV 1 and one fixed reward location throughout the 14\\\\u2009days (Extended Data Fig. 1d,f).\\\\n\\\\nWe targeted 80\\u2013100 trials per session with simultaneous 2P imaging (described below), with one imaging session per day (mean\\\\u2009\\u00b1\\\\u2009s.d., 80.5\\\\u2009\\u00b1\\\\u20097.4 trials across 14 mice, all imaging days). The session was terminated early if the mouse ceased licking or running consistently and/or if the imaging time exceeded 50\\\\u2009min. Before the imaging session, mice were provided 30 \\u2018warm-up\\u2019 trials using the task and reward zone from the previous day to re-acclimate them to the VR setup (using the pre-training environment on day\\\\u20091 of imaging). Following each daily imaging session, mice were given another ~100 training trials without imaging on the last reward zone seen in the imaging session until they acquired their daily water allotment.\\\\n\\\\n# 2P imaging\\\\n\\\\nWe used a resonant-galvo scanning 2P microscope from Neurolabware with Scanbox software (https://scanbox.org, v.4.7) operated through MATLAB (Mathworks) to image the calcium activity of CA1 neurons. Excitation was achieved using 920\\\\u2009nm light from a tunable output femtosecond pulsed laser (Coherent Chameleon Discovery). Laser power modulation was accomplished with a Pockels cell (Conoptics). The typical excitation power, measured at the front face of the objective (Leica \\u00d725, 1.0\\\\u2009NA, 2.6\\\\u2009mm working distance), ranged from 15\\u201368\\\\u2009mW for mice m2\\u2013m7, 50\\u2013100\\\\u2009mW for m10 and m11, and 13\\u201340\\\\u2009mW for m12\\u201319. The task was imaged starting from day\\\\u20091 for all mice except m11, for whom imaging started on day\\\\u20093 owing to lower viral expression. For most animals and sessions, to minimize photodamage and photobleaching, the Pockels cell was used to reduce laser power to minimal levels between trials (during the teleport period), except for mice m11\\u2013m14 on task days 1, 7, 8 and 14, and mice m15\\u2013m19 on day\\\\u20091 and all switch days, for which laser power was maintained and imaging continued throughout the teleport period. Photons were collected using Hamamatsu gated GAsP photomultiplier tubes (part H11706-401 for Neurolabware Microscopes). The imaging field of view (FOV) was collected at 1.0 magnification with unidirectional scanning at ~15.5\\\\u2009Hz, resulting in an approximately 0.7\\\\u2009\\u00d7\\\\u20090.7\\\\u2009mm FOV (512\\\\u2009\\u00d7\\\\u2009796 pixels). An electrically tunable lens was used to simultaneously image deep and superficial CA1 in two mice (m17 and m18), in two planes separated by ~27\\\\u2009\\u00b5m, with frames bidirectionally imaged at ~31\\\\u2009Hz interleaved in the scan for a sampling rate of ~15.5\\\\u2009Hz per plane. In all mice, the same FOV was acquired each session by aligning to a reference image from previous days before the start of data acquisition, with the aid of the \\u2018searchref\\u2019 plugin in Scanbox. This allowed us to track single cells across days.\\\\n\\\\n# Calcium data processing\\\\n\\\\nThe Suite2P software package (v.0.10.3) was used to perform xy motion correction (rigid and non-rigid) and identify putative cell regions of interest (ROIs). Manual curation eliminated ROIs containing multiple somata or dendrites, lacking visually obvious transients, suspected of overexpressing the calcium indicator or exhibiting high and continuous fluorescence fluctuation typical of putative interneurons. This approach yielded 155\\u20132172 putative pyramidal neurons per session, owing to variation in imaging window implant quality and viral expression. In multi-plane imaged animals (see \\u20182P imaging\\u2019), ROIs were identified separately per plane, but planes were pooled for all analyses except those in Extended Data Fig. 7. Custom code was used to follow individual ROIs across imaging sessions using the intersection over union of their pixels. The threshold for ROI matching was chosen algorithmically for each dataset such that the intersection over union for the best match for an ROI pair was always greater than the intersection over union for any second-best match.\\\\n\\\\nTo compute the \\u0394F/F (dF/F) for each ROI, baseline fluorescence was calculated within each trial independently using a maximin procedure with a 20\\\\u2009s sliding window. Limitation to individual trials both accounts for potential photobleaching over the session and avoids the teleport periods for sessions during which the laser power was reduced (see \\u20182P imaging\\u2019). The value of dF/F was then calculated for each cell as the fluorescence minus the baseline, divided by the absolute value of the baseline, then smoothed with a two-sample (~0.129\\\\u2009s) s.d. Gaussian kernel. The activity rate was extracted by deconvolving dF/F with a canonical calcium kernel using the OASIS algorithm as used in Suite2p. We note that this deconvolution is not interpreted as a spike rate but rather as a method to eliminate the asymmetric smoothing of the calcium signal induced by indicator kinetics. Additional putative interneurons were detected for exclusion from further analysis by a Pearson correlation of >0.5 between their dF/F timeseries and the animal\\u2019s running speed, excluding 0.42\\\\u2009\\u00b1\\\\u20090.85% of cells (mean\\\\u2009\\u00b1\\\\u2009s.d. across mice and days).\\\\n\\\\n# Statistics and reproducibility\\\\n\\\\nTo avoid assumptions about data distributions, we used nonparametric tests or permutation tests in most cases, except when a Shapiro\\u2013Wilk test for normality confirmed that parametric tests were reasonable. In the main text, percentages of cells classified by remapping type are reported as mean\\\\u2009\\u00b1\\\\u2009s.d. out of all place cells identified on the specified days. Cells were excluded from certain analyses only if they did not meet pre-established criteria as \\u2018place cells\\u2019 (defined in \\u2018Place cell identification\\u2019), to ensure that included cells provided reliable information about position in the task. Averaged data in figures are shown as mean\\\\u2009\\u00b1\\\\u2009s.e.m. unless otherwise indicated. For the distributions of sequence positions over days in Fig. 4 and Extended Data Fig. 4, s.e.m. shading is omitted for clarity, but the variance for these distributions is accounted for in linear mixed-effects models to quantify these plots (see \\u2018Linear mixed-effects models\\u2019). For data displayed as violin plots, the shape is computed as a kernel density estimate to the bounds of the data, the solid center line marks the median and additional horizontal dashed lines mark the inner quartile.\\\\n\\\\nAll analyses were performed in Python (v.3.8.5). Linear mixed-effects models were performed using the mixedlm method of the statsmodels package (https://www.statsmodels.org/stable/mixed_linear.html). Pairwise repeated measures t-tests were run using the pingouin package (https://pingouin-stats.org) with a Holm step-down correction for multiple comparisons. Linear regressions and Wilcoxon signed-rank and rank-sum tests were performed using the SciPy (https://scipy.org) statistics module, with linear regression confidence intervals computed with the uncertainties package (https://pypi.org/project/uncertainties). Circular statistics were performed using Astropy (https://docs.astropy.org/en/stable/stats/circ.html), pycircstat (https://github.com/circstat/pycircstat) and circular\\u2013circular correlation code originally written to analyze hippocampal theta phase precession (https://github.com/CINPLA/phase-precession). The time warp models, GLM and factorized k-means were implemented from publicly available repositories (time warp, https://github.com/ahwillia/affinewarp; GLM, https://github.com/HarveyLab/GLM_Tensorflow_2; factorized k-means, https://github.com/ahwillia/lvl).\\\\n\\\\nThe number of mice to include was determined by having coverage of every possible reward sequence permutation (six sequences) with at least one mouse in the switch task. In addition, our mouse sample sizes were similar to those reported in previous publications. Mice were randomly selected to experience the switch task (n\\\\u2009=\\\\u200911 mice) versus the \\u2018fixed-condition\\u2019 task in which the reward zone was not moved (n\\\\u2009=\\\\u20093).\\\\n\\\\n# Estimation of anatomical location per ROI\\\\n\\\\nAP and ML ROI coordinates were computed as the 2D center of mass of ROI pixels, relative to each FOV. In multi-plane imaged animals, ROIs were separated according to their deep or superficial imaging plane. To estimate deep-superficial (dorso-ventral (DV)) coordinates in single-plane animals, we acquired a z-stack scan after the day\\\\u200914 task session that extended \\u2212100\\\\u2009\\u00b5m below (\\u221260\\\\u2009\\u00b5m below in one mouse) to +100\\\\u2009\\u00b5m above the FOV in the DV axis, in 2\\\\u2009\\u00b5m steps with ~50 frames per step. Each step was independently motion-corrected as described above, and the mean of each step was used to compile the 3D z-stack array. We then took the AP projection of each ML slice of the z-stack (that is, looking at the side view of the z-stack). We minimum-filtered (4\\\\u2009\\u00d7\\\\u20090 pixel window, ML\\\\u2009\\u00d7\\\\u2009DV) and smoothed (2\\\\u2009\\u00d7\\\\u20092 pixel s.d. Gaussian) each slice, then detected the most prominent dip in brightness in the DV axis to approximate the location of cell bodies, under the assumption that the GCaMP signal is typically absent in the nuclei and brighter in the surrounding neuropil. This yielded a per-pixel estimate of the depth of the pyramidal layer center from the dorsal surface of the z-stack, which we then smoothed (40\\\\u2009\\u00d7\\\\u200940 pixel s.d. Gaussian) to approximate the cell layer \\u2018curvature\\u2019 across the FOV. The curvature estimate was registered to the mean FOV of each imaging session. Given that the imaging plane transects this curvature, calculating the mean DV distance of each ROI (across pixels) from the curvature provides an estimate of where each ROI resides in the deep-to-superficial axis.\\\\n\\\\n# Quantification of licking behavior\\\\n\\\\nThe capacitive lick sensor allowed us to detect single licks. A very small number of trials with erroneous lick detection from damage to the circuit were removed from subsequent licking analysis by setting these values to NaN (~0.65% of all imaged trials, n\\\\u2009=\\\\u200981 out of 12,376 trials removed across 11 switch mice). These trials were detected by >30% of the 0.0645\\\\u2009s imaging frame samples in the trial containing a cumulative lick count >2, as this would have produced a sustained lick rate \\u226520\\\\u2009Hz. Remaining lick counts were converted to a binary vector and spatially binned at the same resolution as neural activity (10\\\\u2009cm bins), then divided by the time occupancy in each bin to yield a lick rate. We quantified licking precision over blocks of ten trials using an anticipatory lick ratio, computed as:\\\\n\\\\n\\\\\\\\rm{Lick}\\\\\\\\; \\\\\\\\rm{ratio}=\\\\\\\\frac{\\\\\\\\rm{Lic}\\\\\\\\rm{k}_{\\\\\\\\rm{in}}-\\\\\\\\rm{Lic}\\\\\\\\rm{k}_{\\\\\\\\rm{out}}}{\\\\\\\\rm{Lic}\\\\\\\\rm{k}_{\\\\\\\\rm{in}}+\\\\\\\\rm{Lic}\\\\\\\\rm{k}_{\\\\\\\\rm{out}}}\\\\n\\\\nwhere Lickin is the mean lick rate in a 50\\\\u2009cm \\u2018anticipatory\\u2019 zone before the start of the reward zone and Lickout is the mean rate outside of this zone and the reward zone. The reward zone itself is thus excluded to exclude consummatory licks (Extended Data Fig. 1e). A ratio of 1 indicates perfect licking only in the anticipatory zone, a ratio of \\u22121 indicates licking only outside of this zone and a ratio of 0 indicates chance licking everywhere, excluding the reward zone.\\\\n\\\\n# Place cell identification\\\\n\\\\nFor all neural spatial activity analyses, we excluded activity when the animal was moving at <2\\\\u2009cm\\\\u2009s\\u22121. To obtain an activity matrix of trials\\\\u2009\\u00d7\\\\u2009position bins for each cell, we binned the 450\\\\u2009cm linear track into 45 bins of 10\\\\u2009cm each. We took the mean dF/F or deconvolved calcium activity on each trial within each position bin (note that taking the mean activity over time samples is equivalent to normalizing by the occupancy within a trial).\\\\n\\\\nWe defined place cells as cells with significant SI65 in either trial set 1 (pre-reward-switch) or trial set 2 (post-reward-switch) in a session. On days without a switch, we used the first and second halves of trials. For the SI, we used the deconvolved calcium activity as this reduces the asymmetry of the calcium signal. SI was calculated for calcium imaging as:\\\\n\\\\n{{\\\\\\\\rm{SI}}}=\\\\\\\\mathop{\\\\\\\\sum }\\\\\\\\limits_{i=1}^{N}{p}_{i}\\\\\\\\frac{{f}_{i}}{f}{\\\\\\\\rm{log}}_{2}\\\\\\\\frac{{f}_{i}}{f},\\\\n\\\\nwhere pi is the occupancy probability in position bin i for the whole session, fi is the trial-averaged activity per position bin i and f is the mean activity over the whole session, computed as the sum of fi\\\\u2009\\u00d7\\\\u2009pi over all N bins. To determine pi, we calculated the per-trial occupancy (number of imaging samples) in each bin divided by the total number of samples per trial, then summed the occupancy probabilities across trials and divided by the total per session. To determine the significance of the SI scores, we created a null distribution by circularly permuting the position data relative to the timeseries of each cell, by a random amount between ~1\\\\u2009s and the length of the trial, independently on each trial. SI was calculated from the trial-averaged activity of each shuffle, and this shuffle procedure was repeated 100 times per cell. A cell\\u2019s true SI was considered significant if it exceeded 95% of the SI scores from all shuffles within an animal, pooled across cells (more stringent than comparing to the shuffle of each individual cell). For plotting place cell firing over trials (for example, Fig. 2), deconvolved calcium activity was normalized to the mean of each cell within a session and smoothed with a 10\\\\u2009cm\\\\u2009s.d. Gaussian.\\\\n\\\\n# Spatial peak firing identification\\\\n\\\\nTo identify spatial peak firing of place cells, we used the position bin of the maximum unsmoothed, spatially binned dF/F, as this signal is the closest to the raw data, averaged across trials within a set (pre-switch or post-switch). =====\\\\n# Preprocessing guide\\\\n\\\\nThis is meant to serve as a reference for how data were preprocessed from the raw imaging/VR files to the several levels of data structures used for analysis.\\\\n\\\\nAll file paths are written as if we are in the current repo, i.e. the pip installable modules will be listed as `./src/reward_relative`  \\\\\\\\\\\\nIf I say \\\"module\\\" I mean to look for a .py file in `./src/reward_relative`  \\\\\\\\\\\\n\\\\n### Directory structure\\\\n\\\\n* data_root: e.g. `/data/`\\\\n    * `2P_Data` (sometimes omitted locally)\\\\n        * animal\\\\n            * date: dd_mm_yyyy\\\\n                * scene: e.g. Env1_LocationA\\\\n                    * If you copied the scanbox files locally, they would be here as .sbx and .mat alongside the below directory\\\\n                    * session + scan directory: e.g. Env1_LocationA_002_001; session (index of Unity VR file) = 002, scan number (index of scan in Scanbox) = 001\\\\n                        * suite2p\\\\n                            * plane0 (plane1, combined, etc if multiplane)\\\\n    * `VR_Data`\\\\n        * animal\\\\n            * date: dd_mm_yyy\\\\n                * SQLite files: scene_session.sqlite, e.g. Env1_LocationA_2.sqlite\\\\n\\\\n### Order of operations:\\\\n\\\\n0. Update `./src/reward_relative/sessions_dict.py` with the metadata from each recording session, with one dictionary entry per animal in either `single_plane` or `multi_plane`\\\\n    1. Include date, scene name, session, scan number, experiment day (1-indexed)\\\\n1. Extract binaries, run suite2p to do motion correction and find ROIs\\\\n    1. In `./notebooks/suite2p_notebooks`, duplicate an existing notebook and rename for the current animal\\\\n    2. Manually edit with the sessions you want to process\\\\n    3. Manually edit any suite2p parameters you want to change (for example, if you think the expression was weak, you could lower threshold_scaling from 1 to 0.6-0.8)\\\\n    4. For Scanbox data, running the main notebook cell will convert the .sbx files to .h5 and then run suite2p\\\\n2. Curate cells in the suite2p GUI\\\\n    1. `iscell.npy` is automatically updated and saved during curation\\\\n    2. Be sure to backup curated data so you don\\\\'t lose all that work!\\\\n3. Copy VR_Data to the local machine with rclone or similar\\\\n4. Make `sess` class which aligns imaging data to Unity VR data.\\\\n    1. The code for the class object itself is in a separate repo, `TwoPUtils` \\\\n    2. The function that actually interpolates/aligns VR data to the slower imaging frame rate is `vr_align_to_2P` in `TwoPUtils/preprocessing.py`\\\\n    3. Use `./notebooks/make_session_pkl.ipynb` to make sess for each animal and list of experiment days that you want.\\\\n    4. `sess` pickles will be saved in `/data_root/sess/`\\\\n    5. Note `sess` does not contain deltaF/F -- this happens next, because it is more customizable. The idea is that you should only ever have to make the `sess` class once.\\\\n5. Make `multi_anim_sess` dictionary to collect and post-process sess across animals.\\\\n    1. Use `./notebooks/make_multi_anim_sess.ipynb`\\\\n    2. The core function `multi_anim_sess` in the `utilities` module runs functions to compute deltaF/F (dFF), calculates place cells from shuffles, and adds details like a trial set dictionary.\\\\n        1. The function that actually computes dFF is `dff` in the `preprocessing` module\\\\n    2. All of these are collected with the sess data for multiple animals on a single day, and stored in a dictionary where each top entry is an animal name. \\\\n    3. The notebook then saves this `multi_anim_sess` as a pickle so that we can work off a constant set of place cell IDs.\\\\n        1. TO-DO to free up disk space: save the sess pickle path as a pointer rather than re-saving the whole class inside multi_anim_sess\\\\n6. Align ROIs across days (can happen at any time after making sess)\\\\n    1. Use `./notebooks/Run_ROI_Aligner.ipynb`\\\\n    2. Saves matched ROI pair indices in `/data_root/sess/<animal>/roi_aligned_results.pkl`\\\\n7. For most analyses in Sosa, Plitt, Giocomo 2024, it is then necessary to create a `dayData` class for each experiment day, across animals.\\\\n    1. Use `./notebooks/make_multiDayData_class.ipynb`. `./reward_relative/dayData` has its own module with some helper functions. This `dayData` class contains computationally-intensive results like identifying reward-relative cells compared to a trial-by-trial shuffle of their activity.\\\\n8. Run remaining analyses and plot figures in `./notebooks` (more notebook figures will be posted as they are cleaned up for readability and documented).\\\\n\\\\n\\\\n\\\\n===== 2 =====\\\\nimport numpy as np\\\\nimport scipy as sp\\\\nimport pandas as pd\\\\nfrom datetime import datetime\\\\nfrom glob import glob\\\\nimport os.path\\\\nimport warnings\\\\n\\\\nfrom sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression as linreg\\\\nimport TwoPUtils\\\\nimport TwoPUtils.utilities as u\\\\nfrom TwoPUtils.utilities import nansmooth\\\\n\\\\nfrom . import utilities as ut\\\\nfrom suite2p.extraction import dcnv\\\\n\\\\ndef create_sess(basedir, scandir, vrdir, animal, date, scene, session, scan_number,\\\\n                load_scaninfo=True,\\\\n                load_VR=True,\\\\n multiple animals\\\\n\\\\n    Attributes:\\\\n \\\\n    anim_list: list of mouse IDs included in this day\\\\n    experiment: experiment name (\\\\'MetaLearn\\\\')\\\\n    place_cell_logical: \\\\'or\\\\' -- cells were classified as place cells by having \\\\n        significant spatial information in the trials before OR after the reward switch\\\\n    force_two_sets: True -- trials were split into \\\"set 0\\\" for \\\"before\\\" the \\\\n        reward switch, and \\\"set 1\\\" for after the reward switch. In animals without a reward switch, \\\\n        \\\"set 0\\\" and \\\"set 1\\\" correspond to the 1st and 2nd half of trials, respectively\\\\n    ts_key: \\\\'dff\\\\' -- timeseries data type (dF/F) used to find place cell peaks\\\\n    use_speed_thr: True -- whether a running speed threshold was used to quantify neural activity\\\\n    speed_thr: 2 -- the speed threshold used, in cm/s\\\\n    exclude_int: True -- whether putative interneurons were excluded from analyses\\\\n    int_thresh: 0.5 -- speed correlation threshold to identify putative interneurons\\\\n    int_method: \\\\'speed\\\\' -- method of finding putative interneurons\\\\n    reward_dist_exclusive: 50 -- distance in cm to exclude cells \\\"near\\\" reward\\\\n    reward_dist_inclusive: 50 -- distance in cm to include cells as \\\"near\\\" reward\\\\n    reward_overrep_dist: 25 -- distance in cm for population-level quantification of reward over-representation\\\\n    activity_criterion: False -- optional additional place cell criterion based on fraction of trials active\\\\n    bin_size: 10 -- linear bin size (cm) for quantifying spatial activity\\\\n    sigma: 1 -- Gaussian s.d. in bins for smoothing\\\\n    smooth: False -- whether to smooth for finding place cell peaks\\\\n    impute_NaNs: True -- whether to impute NaN bins in spatial activity matrices\\\\n    sim_method: \\\\'correlation\\\\' -- trial-by-trial similarity matrix method: \\\\'cosine_sim\\\\' or \\\\'correlation\\\\'\\\\n    lick_correction_thr: 0.35 -- threshold to detect capacitive sensor errors and set trial licking to NaN\\\\n    is_switch: whether each animal had a reward switch\\\\n    anim_tag: string of animal ID numbers\\\\n    trial_dict: dictionary of booleans identified each trial as in \\\"set 0\\\" or \\\"set 1\\\"\\\\n    rzone_pos: [start, stop] position of each reward zone (cm)\\\\n    rzone_by_trial: same as above but for each trial\\\\n    rzone_label: label of each reward zone (e.g. \\\\'A\\\\')\\\\n    blocks: 10-trial blocks within each trial set\\\\n    activity_matrix: spatially-binned activity of type ts_key, trials x position bins x neurons \\\\n    events: original spatially-binned deconvolved events, trials x position bins x neurons (no speed threshold applied)\\\\n    place_cell_masks: booleans of length cells identifying which cells are place cells in each trial set\\\\n    SI: spatial information for each cell in each trial set\\\\n    overall_place_cell_masks: boolean of length cells identifying which cells are place cells according to place_cell_logical\\\\n    peaks: spatial bin center of peak activity for each cell in each trial set\\\\n    field_dict: dictionary of place field properties for each cell\\\\n    plane_per_cell: imaging plane of each cell (all zeros if only a single plane was imaged, \\\\n        otherwise 0 or 1 if two planes were imaged)\\\\n    is_int: boolean, whether each cell is a putative interneuron\\\\n    is_reward_cell: boolean, whether each cell has a peak within 50 cm of both reward zone starts\\\\n    is_end_cell: boolean, whether each cell has a peak in the first or last spatial bin of the track\\\\n    is_track_cell: boolean, whether each cell\\\\'s peak stays within 50 cm of itself from trial set 0 to trial set 1\\\\n    pc_distr: binned distribution of place cell peaks along the track\\\\n    rew_frac: fraction of place cells with a peak within reward_overrep_dist of the reward zone start\\\\n    rate_map: mean deconvolved activity across the population of cells in each spatial bin\\\\n    pv_sim_mean: mean of the population vector similarity matrix in each trial block\\\\n    sim_to_set0: similarity of the population vector in each block to its activity in set 0\\\\n    sim_mat: trial-by-trial similarity matrix for place cells, licking, and speed\\\\n    curr_zone_lickrate: lickrate in the current reward zone by trial\\\\n    other_zone_lickrate: lickrate in the non-active/previous reward zone by trial\\\\n    curr_vs_other_lickratio: ratio of lickrate in the current reward zone vs. the other reward zone\\\\n    in_vs_out_lickratio: ratio of lickrate in the anticipatory zone vs. everywhere outside the anticipatory and reward zones\\\\n    lickpos_std: standard deviation of licking position\\\\n    lickpos_com: center of mass of licking position\\\\n    lick_mat: matrix of lick rate in each spatial bin by trial\\\\n    def_block_by: how to define trial blocks\\\\n    cell_class: dictionary containing booleans of which cells have remapping types classified\\\\n        as \\\"track-relative\\\", \\\"disappear\\\", \\\"appear\\\", \\\"reward\\\", or \\\"nonreward_remap\\\" \\\\n        (see spatial.get_cell_classes)\\\\n    pos_bin_centers: position bin centers\\\\n    dist_btwn_rel_null: distance between spatial firing peaks before the switch \\\\n        and the \\\"random Sample from all trials\\\\n        ntrials_per_session = [len(data[\\\\'neural\\\\'][i]) for i in range(nsessions)]\\\\n        valid_trials_per_session = [np.arange(n) for n in ntrials_per_session]\\\\n    else:\\\\n        # Sample only from specified trial indices\\\\n        ntrials_per_session = [len(trial_indices[i]) for i in range(nsessions)]\\\\n        valid_trials_per_session = trial_indices\\\\n\\\\n    # Sample sessions with equal probability\\\\n    sessionplot = np.random.choice(nsessions, nsample, replace=nsample > nsessions)\\\\n\\\\n    trialplot = np.full((nsample,), -1, dtype=int)\\\\n    for session in range(nsessions):\\\\n        idx = session == sessionplot\\\\n        ncurr = np.count_nonzero(idx)\\\\n        if ncurr == 0:\\\\n            continue\\\\n        valid_trials = valid_trials_per_session[session]\\\\n        if len(valid_trials) == 0:\\\\n            continue\\\\n        # Sample from valid trials and map back to original trial indices\\\\n        sampled = np.random.choice(len(valid_trials), ncurr, replace=len(valid_trials) < ncurr)\\\\n        trialplot[idx] = valid_trials[sampled]\\\\n\\\\n    return sessionplot, trialplot\\\\n    \\\\n\\\\ndef _prepare_session_data(neural: list, input: list, output: list, device, doutput: int = None):\\\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\\\n    Helper function to prepare session data for training/validation.\\\\n\\\\n    Inputs:\\\\n        neural: list of length nsessions, neural[session] is a list of trials\\\\n        input: list of length nsessions, input[session] is a list of trials\\\\n        output: list of length nsessions, output[session] is a list of trials\\\\n        device: torch device to move data to\\\\n        doutput: number of output dimensions (if None, inferred from data)\\\\n\\\\n    Returns:\\\\n        session_data: list of dicts with \\\\'neural\\\\', \\\\'input\\\\', \\\\'output\\\\', \\\\'nneurons\\\\' tensors\\\\n        dinput: input dimension\\\\n        doutput: output dimension\\\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\\\n    nsessions = len(neural)\\\\n\\\\n    # Throughput peaks where the thread count\\\\n# matches the CPUs the container is allowed and falls away on both sides: measured\\\\n# against one thread, 8 threads gave 5.7x, 16 gave 9.0x, 24 gave 7.0x and 32 gave\\\\n# 5.8x. Past the limit the threads exhaust the cgroup\\\\'s CPU budget early in each\\\\n# period and are stopped until the next one, resuming with cold caches, which this\\\\n# strided copy is especially sensitive to.\\\\nCONVERSION_WORKERS = 16\\\\n\\\\n\\\\nclass SessionData(torch.utils.data.Dataset):\\\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Per-session tensors, converted on demand instead of all at once.\\\\n\\\\n    Indexed like the list this used to return: ``session_data[session][\\\\'neural\\\\']``.\\\\n    Converts on every call, so bind it once per session:\\\\n    ``batch = session_data[session]``.\\\\n\\\\n    Why: the previous version built every session\\\\'s float32 tensors up front while\\\\n    the caller\\\\'s numpy arrays stayed alive -- two full copies of the dataset. NotImplementedError\\\\n    elif neuropil_method is None:\\\\n        pass\\\\n    else:\\\\n        print(\\\\'Undefined neuropil_method\\\\')\\\\n        raise NotImplementedError\\\\n\\\\n    print(\\\\'No minimum subtraction\\\\')\\\\n\\\\n    # Caluclate baseline for chan 1\\\\n    flow = np.zeros(f_.shape) * np.nan\\\\n    spks = np.zeros(f_.shape) * np.nan\\\\n\\\\n    # get baseline within each trial\\\\n    for i, (start, stop) in enumerate(zip(start_inds, stop_inds)):\\\\n\\\\n        # if we subtracted neuropil, add back in the neuropil mean in each trial\\\\n        # such that dff values will be close to true \\u2206F/F, and we don\\\\'t divide by small numbers\\\\n        if neuropil_method == \\\\'subtract\\\\':\\\\n            f_[:, start-1:stop-1] = f_[:, start-1:stop-1] + neu_coef * np.nanmean(\\\\n                f_neu_[:, start-1:stop-1], axis=1, keepdims=True)\\\\n\\\\n        if baseline_method == \\\\'maximin\\\\':\\\\n            # cut out ITIs sec\\\\n            flow[:, start-1:stop-1] = sp.ndimage.filters.minimum_filter1d(\\\\n                flow[:, start-1:stop-1], int(300), axis=-1)\\\\n            flow[:, start-1:stop-1] = sp.ndimage.filters.maximum_filter1d(\\\\n                flow[:, start-1:stop-1], int(300), axis=-1)  # max filter with same window (dilation)\\\\n        elif baseline_method == \\\\'maxsmooth\\\\':\\\\n            flow[:, start-1:stop -\\\\n                 1] = nansmooth(f_[:, start-1:stop-1], [0, stop-start])\\\\n        else:\\\\n            print(\\\\'Undefined baseline_method\\\\')\\\\n            raise NotImplementedError\\\\n\\\\n    # to get deltaF/F: subtract baseline from initial signal, divide by abs(baseline)\\\\n    # baseline can some times end up / np.abs(flow[:, nanmask])\\\\n    else:\\\\n        print(\\\\'divide baseline only\\\\')\\\\n        dff[:, nanmask] = (f_[:, nanmask]) / (np.abs(flow[:, nanmask])) - 1\\\\n\\\\n    print(\\\\'dff:\\\\', dff.shape, \\\\'min \\\\', np.nanmin(dff), \\\\'max \\\\', np.nanmax(dff))\\\\n\\\\n    # Smooth the deltaF/F transients by 2 time bins\\\\n    for i, (start, stop) in               (frame_rate/n_planes)\\\\n                                                 )\\\\n\\\\n    if deconvolve:\\\\n        return dff, spks\\\\n    else:\\\\n        return dff\\\\n\\\\n            else:\\\\n                self.peaks[an][\\\\'set \\\\' + s] = spatial.peak(\\\\n                    np.nanmean(\\\\n       for b in bins:\\\\n                occupancy_0 = np.sum(set0_idx[speed_idx == b])\\\\n                occupancy_1 = np.sum(set1_idx[speed_idx == b])\\\\n                occupancy_all = bins=tinybins, ax=ax[0], fill=True, stat=\\\\'probability\\\\')\\\\n# bottom = last switch day\\\\nsns.histplot(data=relpos_deliv[14], bins=tinybins, ax=ax[1], fill=True, stat=\\\\'probability\\\\')\\\\n\\\\nax[0].set_xlim([-np.pi, np.pi])\\\\n\\\\n\\\\n===== 2 =====\\\\n# Extended documentation for data stored in multi_anim_sess\\\\n\\\\nAs an example, let\\\\'s take: multi_anim_sess file 2-3-4-6-7-10-11-12-13-14-15-17-18-19_expday3_speed2_perms100_maximin_events.pickle.\\\\n\\\\nThis filename means:\\\\n\\\\n* it includes mice m2, m3, m4, m6, m7, m10, m11, m12, m12, m13, m14, m15, m17, m18, m19. 2,3,4,7,11,12,13,14,15,17,18,19 learned the reward zone switch task.\\\\n* it contains data from experiment day 3 (the 1st switch day)\\\\n* place cells were identified taking neural activity at running speeds >2 cm/s\\\\n* 100 permutations were used for place cell significance calculations\\\\n* the dF/F baseline was calculated using a `maximin` filter\\\\n* deconvolved calcium `events` were used to define place cells\\\\n\\\\nEach pickle file is a collection of dictionaries, with a key for each animal, plus a key for the `params` used to define place cells. Animals were originally named by indicator (i.e. GCAMP) and a number.\\\\n\\\\nIf you call the entry for a given animal, say \\\"GCAMP4\\\", it is a dictionary with keys: \\\\n```\\\\ndict_keys([\\\\'animal\\\\', \\\\'sess\\\\', \\\\'pc masks set0\\\\', \\\\'pc masks set1\\\\', \\\\'SI set0\\\\', \\\\'SI set1\\\\', \\\\'p set0\\\\', \\\\'p set1\\\\', \\\\'pc perms set0\\\\', \\\\'pc perms set1\\\\', \\\\'SI perms set0\\\\', \\\\'SI perms set1\\\\', \\\\'isreward\\\\', \\\\'morph\\\\', \\\\'dream\\\\', \\\\'rzone\\\\', \\\\'rz label\\\\', \\\\'trial dict\\\\'])\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n* `pc masks setx` is the boolean indicator telling you which unit/cell is a place cell or not in each trial set, where True means p<0.05 that it\\\\'s a place cel\\u20262001 tokens truncated\\u2026(defined in \\u2018Place cell identification\\u2019), to ensure that included cells provided reliable information about position in the task. Averaged data in figures are shown as mean\\\\u2009\\u00b1\\\\u2009s.e.m. unless otherwise indicated. For the distributions of sequence positions over days in Fig. 4 and Extended Data Fig. 4, s.e.m. shading is omitted for clarity, but the variance for these distributions is accounted for in linear mixed-effects models to quantify these plots (see \\u2018Linear mixed-effects models\\u2019). For data displayed as violin plots, the shape is computed as a kernel density estimate to the bounds of the data, the solid center line marks the median and additional horizontal dashed lines mark the inner quartile.\\\\n53:For all neural spatial activity analyses, we excluded activity when the animal was moving at <2\\\\u2009cm\\\\u2009s\\u22121. To obtain an activity matrix of trials\\\\u2009\\u00d7\\\\u2009position bins for each cell, we binned the 450\\\\u2009cm linear track into 45 bins of 10\\\\u2009cm each. We took the mean dF/F or deconvolved calcium activity on each trial within each position bin (note that taking the mean activity over time samples is equivalent to normalizing by the occupancy within a trial).\\\\n55:We defined place cells as cells with significant SI65 in either trial set 1 (pre-reward-switch) or trial set 2 (post-reward-switch) in a session. On days without a switch, we used the first and second halves of trials. For the SI, we used the deconvolved calcium activity as this reduces the asymmetry of the calcium signal. SI was calculated for calcium imaging as:\\\\n59:where pi is the occupancy probability in position bin i for the whole session, fi is the trial-averaged activity per position bin i and f is the mean activity over the whole session, computed as the sum of fi\\\\u2009\\u00d7\\\\u2009pi over all N bins. To determine pi, we calculated the per-trial occupancy (number of imaging samples) in each bin divided by the total number of samples per trial, then summed the occupancy probabilities across trials and divided by the total per session. To determine the significance of the SI scores, we created a null distribution by circularly permuting the position data relative to the timeseries of each cell, by a random amount between ~1\\\\u2009s and the length of the trial, independently on each trial. SI was calculated from the trial-averaged activity of each shuffle, and this shuffle procedure was repeated 100 times per cell. A cell\\u2019s true SI was considered significant if it exceeded 95% of the SI scores from all shuffles within an animal, pooled across cells (more stringent than comparing to the shuffle of each individual cell). For plotting place cell firing over trials (for example, Fig. 2), deconvolved calcium activity was normalized to the mean of each cell within a session and smoothed with a 10\\\\u2009cm\\\\u2009s.d. Gaussian.\\\\n63:To identify spatial peak firing of place cells, we used the position bin of the maximum unsmoothed, spatially binned dF/F, as this signal is the closest to the raw data, averaged across trials within a set (pre-switch or post-switch). There was no restriction to field boundaries, thus allowing cells to have multiple fields with a single identified peak.\\\\n67:Correlation matrices were computed using the spatially binned deconvolved activity on each trial, smoothed with a 20\\\\u2009cm\\\\u2009s.d. Gaussian for single cells and a 10\\\\u2009cm\\\\u2009s.d. Gaussian for population vectors. This resulted in a matrix, A, of j trials\\\\u2009\\u00d7\\\\u2009m position bins per cell. For population vectors, single-cell matrices were horizontally concatenated such that A was j trials\\\\u2009\\u00d7\\\\u2009m position bins\\\\u2009\\u00d7\\\\u2009n cells. Each trial was z-scored across the position axis. For single cells, the trial-by-trial correlation matrix C was computed as:\\\\n79:For stringent statistics on putative RR remapping (Fig. 2c\\u2013i and Extended Data Fig. 2g\\u2013n), we restricted the set of included place cells to require significant SI in the pre-switch and post-switch trial sets in each session. We converted the position timeseries of the animal to periodic coordinates, setting 0\\\\u2009cm on the track to \\u2212\\u03c0 and 450\\\\u2009cm to \\u03c0. Spatial peaks for each cell were re-computed using binned dF/F at a periodic bin size of 2\\u03c0\\\\u2009/\\\\u200945 (corresponding to ~10\\\\u2009cm). Putative TR cells were removed by excluding cells with spatial peaks within ~0.698 radians (50\\\\u2009cm) of each other before versus after the switch. The periodic coordinates were then circularly rotated to align the start of each reward zone at 0, to measure the signed circular distance of spatial peaks relative to the start of each reward zone. For scatter plots of spatial peaks in Fig. 2 and Extended Data Fig. 2, points are jittered by a random amount between \\u2212\\u03c0\\\\u2009/\\\\u2009100 and +\\u03c0\\\\u2009/\\\\u2009100 for visualization only. We measured the circularly wrapped difference between relative peaks after minus before the switch, creating a distribution that can be thought of as orthogonal to the unity line in Fig. 2d,f. We compared this distribution to a \\u2018random-remapping\\u2019 shuffle, generated by maintaining the pre-switch peak of each cell and circularly permuting its post-switch firing 1,000 times by a random offset of 0\\u201344 bins. To define a candidate range of RR remapping variability, we computed the circular difference between the maximum and minimum bin of the distribution of true differences between RR peaks that exceeded the upper 95% of the shuffle, divided by two and averaged across switches. In an initial cohort of n\\\\u2009=\\\\u20097 mice, this produced a mean range of \\u00b10.656 radians (~46.9\\\\u2009cm) around 0, which is captured by a 50\\\\u2009cm threshold given our 10\\\\u2009cm bin size. This threshold was subsequently used to identify candidate RR remapping cells (see below).\\\\n93:Using the spatially binned, deconvolved activity smoothed with a 10\\\\u2009cm\\\\u2009s.d. Gaussian, excluding activity from movement speeds <2\\\\u2009cm\\\\u2009s\\u22121, candidate place fields were identified as regions of \\u226520\\\\u2009cm that exceeded 20% of the maximum of the trial-averaged activity19 (over the 30 pre-switch trials or the final 30 post-switch trials). To include a given field, the cell was required to be significantly active within the field boundaries in at least eight (>25%) of the 30 trials, where \\u2018significantly active\\u2019 was defined as the raw, un-binned deconvolved activity at those positions exceeding its mean +1\\\\u2009s.d. across the 30 trials. For analyses of place field properties before versus after a reward switch, cells were excluded that did not have significant SI or a significant field both before and after the switch. The fraction of cell remapping types meeting these criteria on specific days is reported in Extended Data Fig. 3c. For analyses of place field width and in-field activity rate, cells were further excluded if one of their fields overlapped the first or last spatial bin of the track, as this prevented accurate detection of field boundaries that extended into the teleport zone (which was not imaged for some animals; see \\u20182P imaging\\u2019). Place field width was defined as the last minus the first position bin that crossed the initial 20% activity threshold.\\\\n100:To quantify the degree of backward and forward shifting of place fields following a reward zone switch, we first identified the formation lap (trial) of each field post-switch by adapting a previously established procedure31. Within the bounds of each trial-averaged field \\u00b110\\\\u2009cm after the switch, we identified trials in which the post-switch spatially binned activity exceeded its mean +1\\\\u2009s.d. We then found the first five-trial window with at least three trials above this threshold and took the first active trial in that window as the formation lap31. To compute the field\\u2019s center of mass on each lap with maximum spatial resolution, we used the raw deconvolved activity and position within the bounds of the trial-averaged field. We then subtracted the center of mass on the formation lap from the center of mass of the field\\u2019s mean activity in the last 30 trials of the session to quantify the degree of shift (negative values indicate a backwards shift).\\\\n102:# Decoding of RR position\\\\n104:We implemented a previously developed decoder that uses circular\\u2013linear regression38 to predict the animal\\u2019s position in circular, RR coordinates from the deconvolved calcium event timeseries (at speeds of >2\\\\u2009cm\\\\u2009s\\u22121) of RR, TR or non-RR remapping neurons. The model \\u2018decode score\\u2019 at each timepoint t is given by \\\\\\\\cos ({{y}_{t}}-\\\\\\\\hat{{y}}_{t}), where is the animal\\u2019s true RR position and \\\\\\\\hat{{y}}_{t} is the predicted RR position. A score of one indicates perfect prediction and zero indicates random prediction. We performed tenfold cross-validation in which we trained the decoder on 90% of the data and tested it on a held-out 10%, either training and testing within the trials before the switch or training on one set (before) and testing on the other set (after). Figure 3a shows decoder performance from a single cross-validation fold, considering all data from an example session. To quantify decoding accuracy compared to chance, we next downsampled the data to match occupancy at each RR position bin (bin size 2\\u03c0\\\\u2009/\\\\u200945, ~10\\\\u2009cm), either within trials before the switch or in the last 30 trials after the switch (thereby matching trial numbers before and after). We then repeated model training and testing with 100 shuffled datasets for each session and cell subpopulation. In each shuffle, the timeseries of each cell was independently circularly shifted by a uniform random amount up to the number of samples in the session minus one. In Fig. 3b, model performance is reported as a mean decode score over time samples and cross-validation folds compared to the mean of the shuffles for each session. To evaluate decoder performance across sessions, we z-scored the decoder score of each session to its shuffle and binned the z-score as a function of RR position, then averaged across animals (Fig. 3c).\\\\n108:For analysis of behavioral timescale sequential firing of neural subpopulations, we used the unsmoothed, spatially binned dF/F in circular track coordinates (not aligned to reward, although aligning to reward makes no difference for the circular sequence order). We sorted neurons by their peak firing positions from activity averaged over the odd trials before the switch. This sort was cross-validated by plotting the trial-averaged activity of the even trials before the switch (for example, left-hand columns of Fig. 4a, normalized to the mean activity of each cell within a session), then applying this sort to the trials after the switch. The sequence positions before versus after were then taken as the peak firing positions of the trial-averaged \\u2018even-before\\u2019 trials and the trial-averaged \\u2018after\\u2019 trials. Preservation of sequence order was quantified as the circular\\u2013circular correlation coefficient between the sequence positions before versus after. Although a P\\\\u2009value can be directly computed for this correlation coefficient, its significance was further validated by randomly permuting the cell identities of the neurons after the switch and re-computing the correlation coefficient 1,000 times to obtain a null distribution. The observed correlation coefficient, rho, was considered significant if it was outside 95% of the null distribution using a two-tailed test. The P value was calculated as:\\\\n114:For sequences followed across days, we again used the cross-validated sort from trials before the switch (or the first half of trials if reward did not move) on the reference day (the first day in each day pair) and applied this sort to trials after the switch (or second half) on both the reference day and all trials on the target day (the second day in the pair). To measure drift between days, we computed the across-day circular\\u2013circular correlation coefficient between the sequence positions on reference day \\u2018after\\u2019 trials versus target day \\u2018before\\u2019 trials. A minimum of five followed cells in the sequence was required to compute a correlation coefficient.\\\\n116:To quantify the density of sequences, we divided the number of cells with peaks in each 2\\u03c0\\\\u2009/\\\\u200945 position bin by the total number of place cells for that animal. These density curves were smoothed only for visualization with a one-bin s.d. Gaussian and are shown as the mean across animals per each switch day (Fig. 4 and Extended Data Figs. 4 and 5). We visualized the expected uniform distribution as the fraction of place cells in each animal\\u2019s sequence on a given switch day, divided by the number of position bins, averaged across animals.\\\\n124:In the sessions for which we imaged the whole teleport period (see \\u20182P imaging\\u2019), we re-identified place cells including activity during the \\u2018tunnel\\u2019 and part of the \\u2018jitter\\u2019 in the teleport period (see \\u2018Behavioral training and VR tasks\\u2019 for description), and re-categorized remapping types as either TR or RR from this set of place cells. Given that the length of each jitter was different, we only included spatial bins during the jitter that were occupied with fewer than ten trials missing in the session, using spatial bins of 10\\\\u2009cm starting at \\u221250 cm (the start of the tunnel) up to the maximum occupied bin (range, 530\\u2013580\\\\u2009cm from the start of the virtual track at 0\\\\u2009cm). We then re-performed the population sequence analysis (see \\u2018Sequence detection and quantification\\u2019), up to the maximum distance occupied across mice (580\\\\u2009cm); note that some bins during the teleport period will include data from fewer animals (Extended Data Fig. 5). To simulate the \\u2018ideal\\u2019 RR remapping destination if RR fields moved by the exact amount that the reward zone moved, we added the circular difference between reward zone starts to the pre-switch peak activity position of each RR cell.\\\\n132:We first fit five different time warp model types\\u2014shift, linear, piecewise one knot, piecewise two knots and piecewise three knots36\\u2014on the matrix of speed profiles within a trial set (i trials\\\\u2009\\u00d7\\\\u2009j linear position bins\\\\u2009\\u00d7\\\\u20091, expanded in the third dimension for compatibility with the time warp algorithm). These models apply warping functions of increasing nonlinear complexity to stretch and compress the data on each trial for maximal alignment. We included both rewarded and omission trials in the fitting procedure to find the best alignment across them. Model fit was assessed using the mean squared error between the time-warped speed profile of each trial and the mean across time-warped trials (Extended Data Fig. 8a). The piecewise three knots model most often produced the best fit (lowest mean squared error) across sessions (Extended Data Fig. 8c). We therefore re-fit all sessions with piecewise three knots to ensure that model selection could not influence the results. We then applied the model transform to the deconvolved neural activity matrix for the same set of trials (i trials\\\\u2009\\u00d7\\\\u2009j linear position bins\\\\u2009\\u00d7\\\\u2009n neurons; excluding neural data at movement speeds of <2\\\\u2009cm\\\\u2009s\\u22121). For neural analysis in Fig. 6, we then focused on trial sets before the switch in which the reward zone was at position \\u2018A\\u2019 or \\u2018B\\u2019, because the mean rewarded versus omission speed profiles were more similar on these trials than after the switch (Extended Data Fig. 8f) or when the reward was at \\u2018C\\u2019 (as there is less room at the end of the track for running speed to reach its maximum from position \\u2018C\\u2019 on omission trials; see Extended Data Figs. 1 and 8h for examples; see Extended Data Fig. 8 for analyses of trials after the switch and for all reward zones).\\\\n140:where \\\\\\\\bar{{r}}_{j} and \\\\\\\\bar{{o}}_{j} are the mean activity in position bin j averaged across rewarded trials or omission trials, respectively. Reward versus omission indices of 1 indicate an exclusive firing preference for rewarded trials, \\u22121 indicates an exclusive preference for omission trials and 0 indicates equal firing between rewards and omissions. We confirmed that the reward versus omission index calculated from the model-transformed activity was highly correlated with the reward versus omission index calculated from the original activity (Extended Data Fig. 8e). Using a linear mixed-effects model (Extended Data Fig. 8j), we found that there was no significant change in the median reward versus omission index for each animal across days as a result of the model fit or as a result of the original mean squared error between rewarded and omission trials. We therefore combined neurons across days for presentation in Fig. 6e.\\\\n144:We implemented a Poisson GLM developed previously37 to predict the deconvolved calcium event time series of individual neurons from a set of task and movement variables. All behavioral and neural time series were sampled at ~15.5\\\\u2009Hz, the imaging frame rate.\\\\n148:Our design matrix included three task variables and three movement variables (Extended Data Fig. 9a). Task variables were linear track position (from 0 to 450\\\\u2009cm), RR position (from \\u2212\\u03c0 to \\u03c0, centered around the start of each reward zone at 0\\\\u2009radians) and \\u2018rewarded\\u2019, a binary that switches from 0 to 1 when the reward is delivered on each trial and stays at 1 until the end of the trial (stays at 0 on omission trials). Linear track position and RR position were separately expanded into 45 cosine basis functions, one for each 10\\\\u2009cm or ~0.14 radian bin. \\u2018Rewarded\\u2019 was multiplied by the linear position basis functions to yield the interaction between position and whether the animal had been rewarded on a given trial.\\\\n152:All predictors were concatenated into a design matrix with 156 total features: 45 position bases\\\\u2009+\\\\u200945 RR position bases\\\\u2009+\\\\u200945 rewarded-by-position bases\\\\u2009+\\\\u20097 speed bases\\\\u2009+\\\\u20097 acceleration bases\\\\u2009+\\\\u20097 licking bases. Included in Sosa, Plitt, & Giocomo (2025), \\\"A\\\\n  flexible hippocampal population code for experience relative to reward,\\\" Nature\\\\n  Neuroscience.To reinforce rewarding behaviors, events leading up to and following\\\\n  rewards must be remembered. Hippocampal place cell activity spans spatial and non-spatial\\\\n  episodes, but whether hippocampal activity encodes entire sequences of events relative\\\\n  to reward is unknown. Putative interneurons whose trial-period dF/F has\\\\n+  Pearson r > 0.5 with speed are then removed, as described in Methods.\\\\n+* Trials run from each ``trial_start`` sample up to (not including) its matching\\\\n+  ``teleport`` sample. Teleport periods are not included, matching the main paper\\\\n+  analyses. Native aligned samples are retained (~15.5 Hz, 64.48 ms); trials may\\\\n+  consequently have different durations.\\\\n+* The Methods report 81 lick-detector failures as trials on which >30% of samples\\\\n+  had cumulative lick count >2. Those trials are excluded because lick is a\\\\n+  required categorical decoder target and NaN labels cannot be trained on.\\\\n+* Reward outcome requires both a reward delivery timestamp and an active reward-\\\\n+  zone sample, matching behavior.get_trial_types. Previous outcome refers to the\\\\n+  immediately preceding original trial (not merely the preceding retained trial).\\\\n+* Signed distance to the reward zone is negative before the zone, zero anywhere\\\\n+  inside it, and positive after it (distance to the nearest zone boundary).\\\\n+\\\\n+The dual-plane mice have scanner metadata at 62.03 Hz and per-plane series metadata\\\\n+at 31.02 Hz, but their arrays and aligned behavior have one sample per plane frame.\\\\n+The paper states the effective sampling rate is ~15.5 Hz per plane, which also\\\\n+matches the behavioral timestamp spacing. Thus all sessions share the same native\\\\n+time-bin duration.\\\\n+\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n+\\\\n+from __future__ import annotations\\\\n+\\\\n+import argparse\\\\n+import os\\\\n+import pickle\\\\n+import re\\\\n+from pathlib import Path\\\\n+\\\\n+import h5py\\\\n+import numpy as np\\\\n+from scipy.ndimage import gaussian_filter1d, maximum_filter1d, minimum_filter1d\\\\n+from suite2p.extraction import dcnv\\\\n+\\\\n+\\\\n+FRAME_RATE_HZ = 15.5078125\\\\n+NEUROPIL_COEF = 0.7\\\\n+CALCIUM_TAU_S = 0.7\\\\n+BASELINE_WINDOW_SAMPLES = 300  # paper/code: 20 s at approximately 15 Hz\\\\n+LICK_ERROR_FRACTION = 0.30\\\\n+ZONE_BOUNDS = {\\\\\\\"A\\\\\\\": (80.0, 130.0), \\\\\\\"B\\\\\\\": (200.0, 250.0), \\\\\\\"C\\\\\\\": (320.0, 370.0)}\\\\n+\\\\n+\\\\n+def natural_key(path: Path) -> tuple[int, int]:\\\\n+    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"Sort files by numeric mouse and session identifiers.\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n+    match = re.search(r\\\\\\\"sub-m(\\\\\\\\d+).*ses-(\\\\\\\\d+)\\\\\\\", str(path))\\\\n+    if match is None:\\\\n+        raise ValueError(f\\\\\\\"Cannot parse mouse/session from {path}\\\\\\\")\\\\n+    return int(match.group(1)), int(match.group(2))\\\\n+\\\\n+\\\\n+def scene_conditions(scene: str, n_trials: int) -> tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:\\\\n+    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"Return environment (0/1) and reward-zone (A/B/C) for every trial.\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n+    static = re.fullmatch(r\\\\\\\"Env([12])_Location([ABC])\\\\\\\", scene)\\\\n+    same_env_switch = re.fullmatch(r\\\\\\\"Env([12])_Location([ABC])_to_([ABC])\\\\\\\", scene)\\\\n+    env_switch = re.fullmatch(r\\\\\\\"Env([12])_([ABC])_to_Env([12])_([ABC])\\\\\\\", scene)\\\\n+\\\\n+    if static:\\\\n+        environments = np.full(n_trials, int(static.group(1)) - 1, dtype=np.int8)\\\\n+        zones = np.where(np.arange(n_trials) < 30,\\\\n+                         same_env_switch.group(2), same_env_switch.group(3))\\\\n+    elif env_switch:\\\\n+        environments = np.where(np.arange(n_trials) < 30,\\\\n+                                int(env_switch.group(1)) - 1,\\\\n+                                int(env_switch.group(3)) - 1).astype(np.int8)\\\\n+        zones = np.where(np.arange(n_trials) < 30,\\\\n+                         env_switch.group(2), env_switch.group(4))\\\\n+    else:\\\\n+        raise ValueError(f\\\\\\\"Unrecognized scene name: {scene}\\\\\\\")\\\\n+    return environments, zones\\\\n+\\\\n+\\\\n+def discretize_distance(position: np.ndarray, start: float, stop: float) -> np.ndarray:\\\\n+    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"Categorize signed distance to the nearest point in an interval.\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n+    distance = np.where(position < start, position - start,\\\\n+                        np.where(position > stop, position - stop, 0.0))\\\\n+    result = np.empty(distance.shape, dtype=np.int8)\\\\n+    result[distance < -50] = 0\\\\n+    result[(distance >= -50) & (distance < -10)] = 1\\\\n+    result[(distance >= -10) & (distance < 0)] = 2\\\\n+    result[distance == 0] = 3\\\\n+    result[(distance > 0) & (distance <= 10)] = 4\\\\n+    result[(distance > 10) & (distance <= 50)] = 5\\\\n+    result[distance > 50] = 6\\\\n+    return result\\\\n+\\\\n+\\\\n+def compute_dff_and_events(f: np.ndarray, f_neu: np.ndarray) -> tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:\\\\n+    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"Apply the paper repository's single-trial dF/F and deconvolution steps.\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n+    # Inputs are cells x time. Work in float32 to keep the full conversion tractable.\\\\n+    corrected = f - NEUROPIL_COEF * f_neu\\\\n+    corrected += NEUROPIL_COEF * np.mean(f_neu, axis=1, keepdims=True)\\\\n+\\\\n+    baseline = gaussian_filter1d(corrected, 15, axis=1)\\\\n+    baseline = minimum_filter1d(baseline, BASELINE_WINDOW_SAMPLES, axis=1)\\\\n+    baseline = maximum_filter1d(baseline, BASELINE_WINDOW_SAMPLES, axis=1)\\\\n+    with np.errstate(divide=\\\\\\\"ignore\\\\\\\", invalid=\\\\\\\"ignore\\\\\\\"):\\\\n+        dff = (corrected - baseline) / np.abs(baseline)\\\\n+    dff = gaussian_filter1d(dff, 2, axis=1)\\\\n+    if not np.isfinite(dff).all():\\\\n+        # Degenerate zero baselines are not expected in curated ROIs. Preserve the\\\\n+        # usable samples without allowing NaNs/Infs into the decoder.\\\\n+        dff = np.nan_to_num(dff, copy=False)\\\\n+    events = dcnv.oasis(dff, 2000, CALCIUM_TAU_S, FRAME_RATE_HZ)\\\\n+    return dff, np.asarray(events, dtype=np.float32)\\\\n+\\\\n+\\\\n+def read_curated_trial(\\\\n+    h5: h5py.File,\\\\n+    series_name: str,\\\\n+    start: int,\\\\n+    stop: int,\\\\n+    iscell: np.ndarray,\\\\n+    plane_idx: np.ndarray,\\\\n+) -> np.ndarray:\\\\n+    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"Read a cells x time trial, preserving the segmentation table's ROI order.\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n+    pieces: list[np.ndarray] = []\\\\n+    global_indices: list[np.ndarray] = []\\\\n+    series = h5[f\\\\\\\"processing/ophys/{series_name}\\\\\\\"]\\\\n+    for plane_global = np.flatnonzero(plane_idx == plane)\\\\n+        local_keep = np.flatnonzero(iscell[plane_global])\\\\n+        # Reading a contiguous time slab before selecting columns is substantially\\\\n+        # faster in h5py than a large two-dimensional fancy selection.\\\\n+        slab = np.asarray(series[plane_name][\\\\\\\"data\\\\\\\"][start:stop, :], dtype=np.float32)\\\\n+        pieces.append(slab[:, local_keep])\\\\n+        global_indices.append(plane_global[local_keep])\\\\n+\\\\n+    values = np.concatenate(pieces, axis=1)\\\\n+    order = np.argsort(np.concatenate(global_indices))\\\\n+    return np.ascontiguousarray(values[:, order].T)\\\\n+\\\\n+\\\\n+def correlation_from_sums(\\\\n+    n: int,\\\\n+    sx: np.ndarray,\\\\n+    sx2: np.ndarray,\\\\n+    sy: float,\\\\n+    sy2: float,\\\\n+    sxy: np.ndarray,\\\\n+) -> np.ndarray:\\\\n+    numerator = n * sxy - sx * sy\\\\n+    denominator = np.sqrt(np.maximum(0.0, n * sx2 - sx * sx) *\\\\n+                          max(0.0, n * sy2 - sy * sy))\\\\n+    return np.divide(numerator, denominator, out=np.zeros_like(numerator), where=denominator > 0)\\\\n+\\\\n+\\\\n+def np.flatnonzero(behavior[\\\\\\\"trial_start/data\\\\\\\"][:] > 0)\\\\n+        stops = np.flatnonzero(behavior[\\\\\\\"teleport/data\\\\\\\"][:] > 0)\\\\n+        if len(starts) != len(stops) or h5[\\\\\\\"general/session_id\\\\\\\"][()].decode()\\\\n+        environments, zones = scene_conditions(scene, len(starts))\\\\n+\\\\n+        timestamps = behavior[\\\\\\\"lick/data\\\\\\\"][:]\\\\n+        env_stream = behavior[\\\\\\\"environment/data\\\\\\\"][:]\\\\n+        trial_number_stream = behavior[\\\\\\\"trial number/data\\\\\\\"][:]\\\\n+        rzone_stream = behavior[\\\\\\\"reward_zone/data\\\\\\\"][:]\\\\n+        reward_times = behavior[\\\\\\\"Reward/timestamps\\\\\\\"][:]\\\\n+\\\\n+        iscell_table = h5[\\\\\\\"processing/ophys/ImageSegmentation/PlaneSegmentation/iscell\\\\\\\"][:, 0]\\\\n+        iscell = np.asarray(iscell_table == 1)\\\\n+        plane_idx = np.asarray(\\\\n+            h5[\\\\\\\"processing/ophys/ImageSegmentation/PlaneSegmentation/planeIdx\\\\\\\"][:], dtype=int\\\\n+        )\\\\n+        n_curated = int(np.count_nonzero(iscell))\\\\n+\\\\n+        # Determine reward outcomes on all original trials before applying the lick\\\\n+        # quality filter, so \\\\\\\"previous trial\\\\\\\" retains its literal meaning.\\\\n+        outcomes = np.zeros(len(starts), dtype=np.int8)\\\\n+        for trial, (start, stop) in enumerate(zip(starts, stops)):\\\\n+            left = np.searchsorted(reward_times, timestamps[start], side=\\\\\\\"left\\\\\\\")\\\\n+            right = np.searchsorted(reward_times, timestamps[stop], side=\\\\\\\"left\\\\\\\")\\\\n+            outcomes[trial] = int(right > left and np.any(rzone_stream[start:stop] > 0))\\\\n+\\\\n+        trial_events: list[np.ndarray] = []\\\\n+        retained_trial_indices: list[int] = []\\\\n+        # Streaming sufficient statistics for dF/F-speed correlations.\\\\n+        sx = np.zeros(n_curated, dtype=np.float64)\\\\n+        sx2 = np.zeros(n_curated, dtype=np.float64)\\\\n+        sxy = np.zeros(n_curated, dtype=np.float64)\\\\n+        sy = sy2 = 0.0\\\\n+        n_samples = 0\\\\n+        excluded_lick_trials = 0\\\\n+\\\\n+        for trial, (start, stop) in enumerate(zip(starts, stops)):\\\\n+            trial_lick = lick[start:stop]\\\\n+            if np.mean(trial_lick > 2) > LICK_ERROR_FRACTION:\\\\n+                excluded_lick_trials += 1\\\\n+                continue\\\\n+\\\\n+            # The environment stream is a direct source check on scene parsing.\\\\n+            stream_environment = int(round(float(np.median(env_stream[start:stop]))))\\\\n+            if stream_environment != int(environments[trial]):\\\\n+                raise ValueError(f\\\\\\\"Scene/environment mismatch in {path}, trial {trial}\\\\\\\")\\\\n+\\\\n+            f = read_curated_trial(h5, \\\\\\\"Fluorescence\\\\\\\", start, stop, iscell, plane_idx)\\\\n+            f_neu = read_curated_trial(h5, \\\\\\\"Neuropil\\\\\\\", start, stop, iscell, plane_idx)\\\\n+            dff, events = compute_dff_and_events(f, f_neu)\\\\n+            trial_events.append(events)\\\\n+            retained_trial_indices.append(trial)\\\\n+\\\\n+            trial_speed = np.asarray(speed[start:stop], dtype=np.float64)\\\\n+            dff64 = np.asarray(dff, dtype=np.float64)\\\\n+            sx += np.sum(dff64, axis=1)\\\\n+            sx2 += np.sum(dff64 * dff64, axis=1)\\\\n+            sxy += dff64 @ trial_speed\\\\n+            sy += float(np.sum(trial_speed))\\\\n+            sy2 += float(trial_speed @ trial_speed)\\\\n+            n_samples += len(trial_speed)\\\\n+\\\\n+        speed_correlation = correlation_from_sums(n_samples, sx, sx2, sy, sy2, sxy)\\\\n+        keep_neuron = speed_correlation <= 0.5\\\\n+        neural_trials = [np.ascontiguousarray(events[keep_neuron], dtype=np.float32)\\\\n+                         for events in trial_events]\\\\n+\\\\n+        input_trials: list[np.ndarray] = []\\\\n+        output_trials: list[np.ndarray] = []\\\\n+        zone_to_class = {\\\\\\\"A\\\\\\\": 0, \\\\\\\"B\\\\\\\": 1, \\\\\\\"C\\\\\\\": 2}\\\\n+        for trial in retained_trial_indices:\\\\n+            start, stop = starts[trial], stops[trial]\\\\n+            pos = np.asarray(position[start:stop], dtype=np.float32)\\\\n+            spd = np.asarray(speed[start:stop], dtype=np.float32)\\\\n+            time_from_start = np.asarray(timestamps[start:stop] - timestamps[start], dtype=np.float32)\\\\n+            trial_number = float(np.median(trial_number_stream[start:stop]))\\\\n+            previous_outcome = float(outcomes[trial - 1]) if trial > 0 else 0.0\\\\n+            environment = float(environments[trial])\\\\n+            inputs = np.vstack((\\\\n+                time_from_start,\\\\n+                np.full(len(pos), environment, dtype=np.float32),\\\\n+                np.full(len(pos), trial_number, dtype=np.float32),\\\\n+                np.full(len(pos), previous_outcome, dtype=np.float32),\\\\n+            )).astype(np.float32, copy=False)\\\\n+\\\\n+            zone = str(zones[trial])\\\\n+            zone_start, zone_stop = ZONE_BOUNDS[zone]\\\\n+            outputs = np.vstack((\\\\n+                discretize_distance(pos, zone_start, zone_stop),\\\\n+                np.digitize(pos, [90.0, 180.0, 270.0, 360.0]).astype(np.int8),\\\\n+                np.digitize(spd, [2.0, 10.0, 20.0, 40.0]).astype(np.int8),\\\\n+                (lick[start:stop] > 0).astype(np.int8),\\\\n+                np.full(len(pos), zone_to_class[zone], dtype=np.int8),\\\\n+                np.full(len(pos), outcomes[trial], dtype=np.int8),\\\\n+            ))\\\\n+            input_trials.append(np.ascontiguousarray(inputs))\\\\n+            output_trials.append(np.ascontiguousarray(outputs, dtype=np.int8))\\\\n+\\\\n+        info = {\\\\n+            \\\\\\\"source_file\\\\\\\": str(path.relative_to(path.parents[2])),\\\\n+ /app/convert_data.py\\\\n+#!/usr/bin/env python3\\\\n+\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"Convert the Sosa, Plitt & Giocomo NWB release for neural decoding.\\\\n+\\\\n+Decisions tied to the paper/repository\\\\n+--------------------------------------\\\\n+* A session is one NWB recording (one mouse/day); every available session is used.\\\\n+* Only manually curated Suite2p ROIs (``iscell[:, 0] == 1``) are retained.\\\\n+* Neural activity is recomputed from ROI and neuropil fluorescence with the paper's\\\\n+  pipeline: 0.7 neuropil subtraction, trial-local 20 s maximin baseline, dF/F,\\\\n+  Gaussian smoothing (2 imaging samples), and Suite2p OASIS deconvolution with a\\\\n+  0.7 s calcium time constant. Putative interneurons whose trial-period dF/F has\\\\n+  Pearson r > 0.5 with speed are then removed, as described in Methods.\\\\n+* Trials run from each ``trial_start`` sample up to (not including) its matching\\\\n+  ``teleport`` sample. Teleport periods are not included, matching the main paper\\\\n+  analyses. Native aligned samples are retained (~15.5 Hz, 64.48 ms); trials may\\\\n+  consequently have different durations.\\\\n+* The Methods report 81 lick-detector failures as trials on which >30% of samples\\\\n+  had cumulative lick count >2. Those trials are excluded because lick is a\\\\n+  required categorical decoder target and NaN labels cannot be trained on.\\\\n+* Reward outcome requires both a reward delivery timestamp and an active reward-\\\\n+  zone sample, matching behavior.get_trial_types. Previous outcome refers to the\\\\n+  immediately preceding original trial (not merely the preceding retained trial).\\\\n+* Signed distance to the reward zone is negative before the zone, zero anywhere\\\\n+  inside it, and positive after it (distance to the nearest zone boundary).\\\\n+\\\\n+The dual-plane mice have scanner metadata at 62.03 Hz and per-plane series metadata\\\\n+at 31.02 Hz, but their arrays and aligned behavior have one sample per plane frame.\\\\n+The paper states the effective sampling rate is ~15.5 Hz per plane, which also\\\\n+matches the behavioral timestamp spacing. Thus all sessions share the same native\\\\n+time-bin duration.\\\\n+\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n+\\\\n+from __future__ import annotations\\\\n+\\\\n+import argparse\\\\n+import os\\\\n+import pickle\\\\n+import re\\\\n+from pathlib import Path\\\\n+\\\\n+import h5py\\\\n+import numpy as np\\\\n+from scipy.ndimage import gaussian_filter1d, maximum_filter1d, minimum_filter1d\\\\n+from suite2p.extraction import dcnv\\\\n+\\\\n+\\\\n+FRAME_RATE_HZ = 15.5078125\\\\n+NEUROPIL_COEF = 0.7\\\\n+CALCIUM_TAU_S = 0.7\\\\n+BASELINE_WINDOW_SAMPLES = 300  # paper/code: 20 s at approximately 15 Hz\\\\n+LICK_ERROR_FRACTION = 0.30\\\\n+ZONE_BOUNDS = {\\\\\\\"A\\\\\\\": (80.0, 130.0), \\\\\\\"B\\\\\\\": (200.0, 250.0), \\\\\\\"C\\\\\\\": (320.0, 370.0)}\\\\n+\\\\n+\\\\n+def natural_key(path: Path) -> tuple[int, int]:\\\\n+    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"Sort files by numeric mouse and session identifiers.\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n+    match = re.search(r\\\\\\\"sub-m(\\\\\\\\d+).*ses-(\\\\\\\\d+)\\\\\\\", str(path))\\\\n+    if match is None:\\\\n+        raise ValueError(f\\\\\\\"Cannot parse mouse/session from {path}\\\\\\\")\\\\n+    return int(match.group(1)), int(match.group(2))\\\\n+\\\\n+\\\\n+def scene_conditions(scene: str, n_trials: int) -> tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:\\\\n+    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"Return environment (0/1) and reward-zone (A/B/C) for every trial.\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n+    static = re.fullmatch(r\\\\\\\"Env([12])_Location([ABC])\\\\\\\", scene)\\\\n+    same_env_switch = re.fullmatch(r\\\\\\\"Env([12])_Location([ABC])_to_([ABC])\\\\\\\", scene)\\\\n+    env_switch = re.fullmatch(r\\\\\\\"Env([12])_([ABC])_to_Env([12])_([ABC])\\\\\\\", scene)\\\\n+\\\\n+    if static:\\\\n+        environments = np.full(n_trials, int(static.group(1)) - start,\\\\n+                        np.where(position > stop, position - stop, 0.0))\\\\n+    result = np.empty(distance.shape, dtype=np.int8)\\\\n+    result[distance < -50] = 0\\\\n+    result[(distance >= -50) & (distance < -10)] = 1\\\\n+    result[(distance >= -10) & (distance < 0)] = 2\\\\n+    result[distance == 0] = 3\\\\n+    result[(distance > 0) & (distance <= 10)] = 4\\\\n+    result[(distance > 10) & (distance <= 50)] = 5\\\\n+    result[distance > 50] = 6\\\\n+    return result\\\\n+\\\\n+\\\\n+def compute_dff_and_events(f: np.ndarray, f_neu: np.ndarray) -> tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:\\\\n+    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"Apply the paper repository's single-trial dF/F and deconvolution steps.\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n+    # Inputs are cells x time.
