For discrete inputs, use a one-hot encoding.\\\\n        output: variables we want to decode. output is a list of length nsessions, output[session] is a list of length ntrials[session],\\\\n                and output[session][trial] is a numpy array of shape (doutput, T[session][trial]) of dtype = int with\\\\n                categorical values for each timepoint. Each output[idx, t] is an integer from 0 to ncategories[idx]-1.\\\\n        metadata: optional dict with additional information about the experiment.\\\\n\\\\n        Algorithm hyperparameters can be passed as keyword arguments:\\\\n        npcs: number of projected dimensions to use (default: 10)\\\\n        num_epochs: number of training epochs (default: 200)\\\\n        lr: learning rate (default: 0.001)\\\\n        l1_weight: L1 regularization weight for projection matrices (default: 1e-4)\\\\n        svd_max_samples: maximum number of timepoints to use for SVD initialization (default: 50000).\\\\n                         If a session has more timepoints than this, a random subset will be used.\\\\n        svd_max_neurons: maximum number of neurons to use for SVD initialization (default: 2000).\\\\n                         If a session has more neurons than this, random Gaussian projection will be used.\\\\n                         The final weight is the product of the random projection and SVD matrices.\\\\n        ncategories: optional list of length doutput giving number of categories for each output dimension.\\\\n                     If not provided, will be inferred from data.\\\\n        balanced_loss: if True, use class-weighted cross-entropy loss to compensate for class imbalance.\\\\n                       Weights are inverse of class frequency. Default: False.\\\\n\\\\n    Returns:\\\\n        model: dict with trained decoder parameters. dict with the keys:\\\\n            \\\\'projection\\\\': list of length nsessions, projection[session] is a torch tensor of shape (npcs, nneurons[session])\\\\n            \\\\'decoder\\\\': list of length doutput, decoder[out_dim] is a torch linear layer for that output dimension\\\\n            \\\\'ncategories\\\\': list of length doutput, ncategories[out_dim] is the number of categories for that output\\\\n            \\\\'device\\\\': torch device used for training\\\\n\\\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\\\n\\\\n    model = {\\\\n        \\\\'projection\\\\': [],\\\\n        \\\\'decoder\\\\': [],\\\\n        \\\\'ncategories\\\\': [],\\\\n        \\\\'device\\\\': None\\\\n    }\\\\n\\\\n    npcs = kwargs.get(\\\\'npcs\\\\', 10)\\\\n    num_epochs = kwargs.get(\\\\'num_epochs\\\\', 200)\\\\n    lr = kwargs.get(\\\\'lr\\\\', 0.001)\\\\n    l1_weight = kwargs.get(\\\\'l1_weight\\\\', 1e-4)\\\\n    device_str = kwargs.get(\\\\'device\\\\', None)\\\\n    balanced_loss = kwargs.get(\\\\'balanced_loss\\\\', False)\\\\n\\\\n    nsessions = len(neural)\\\\n    if device_str is not None:\\\\n        device = torch.device(device_str)\\\\n    else:\\\\n        device = torch.device(\\\\'cuda\\\\' if torch.cuda.is_available() else \\\\'cpu\\\\')\\\\n\\\\n    model[\\\\'device\\\\'] = device\\\\n    print(\\\\'Using device:\\\\', device)\\\\n\\\\n    # Prepare training data using helper function\\\\n    session_data, dinput, doutput = _prepare_session_data(neural, input, output, device)\\\\n\\\\n    # Determine number of categories for each output dimension\\\\n    if \\\\'ncategories\\\\' in kwargs:\\\\n        ncategories = kwargs[\\\\'ncategories\\\\']\\\\n    else:\\\\n        # Session is the OUTER loop so each one is fetched once for all output\\\\n        # dimensions. With session inner, session_data[session] would rebuild the\\\\n        # same session\\\\'s tensors doutput times. The result does not depend on the\\\\n        # order.\\\\n        max_cat = [0] * doutput\\\\n        for session in range(nsessions):\\\\n            batch = session_data[session]\\\\n            if batch is None:\\\\n                continue\\\\n            for out_dim in range(doutput):\\\\n                max_cat[out_dim] = max(max_cat[out_dim],\\\\n                                       int(batch[\\\\'output\\\\'][:, out_dim].max().item()))\\\\n        ncategories = [m + 1 for m in max_cat]\\\\n\\\\n    model[\\\\'ncategories\\\\'] = ncategories\\\\n\\\\n    # Compute class weights (always, for use in chance computation and optionally for balanced loss)\\\\n    class_weights = []\\\\n    class_fractions = []  # Store fractions for chance computation\\\\n    # Session outer, output dimension inner, for the same reason as ncategories\\\\n    # above: one fetch per session instead of doutput of them. Summation order\\\\n    # within a class count is unchanged.\\\\n    class_counts_all = [np.zeros(ncategories[out_dim]) for out_dim in range(doutput)]\\\\n    for session in range(nsessions):\\\\n        batch = session_data[session]\\\\n        if batch is None:\\\\n            continue\\\\n        for out_dim in range(doutput):\\\\n            outputs = batch[\\\\'output\\\\'][:, out_dim].cpu().numpy()\\\\n            for c in range(ncategories[out_dim]):\\\\n                class_counts_all[out_dim][c] += np.sum(outputs == c)\\\\n\\\\n    for out_dim in range(doutput):\\\\n        class_counts = class_counts_all[out_dim]\\\\n        # Store class fractions for chance computation\\\\n        total = np.sum(class_counts)\\\\n        fractions = class_counts / total\\\\n        class_fractions.append(fractions)\\\\n        # Compute weights as inverse of frequency (with smoothing to avoid division by zero)\\\\n        weights = total / (ncategories[out_dim] * np.maximum(class_counts, 1))\\\\n        # Normalize weights to sum to 1 (so each output dimension is equally weighted)\\\\n        weights = weights / np.sum(weights)\\\\n        class_weights.append(torch.tensor(weights, dtype=torch.float32, device=device))\\\\n\\\\n    model[\\\\'class_weights\\\\'] = class_weights\\\\n    model[\\\\'class_fractions\\\\'] = class_fractions\\\\n\\\\n    if balanced_loss:\\\\n        print(f\\\"Using balanced loss with class weights:\\\")\\\\n        for out_dim in range(doutput):\\\\n            print(f\\\"  Output {out_dim}: {class_weights[out_dim].cpu().numpy()}\\\")\\\\n\\\\n    # Initialize projection matrices for each session\\\\n    svd_max_samples = kwargs.get(\\\\'svd_max_samples\\\\', 50000)  # Max timepoints to use for SVD initialization\\\\n    svd_max_neurons = kwargs.get(\\\\'svd_max_neurons\\\\', 2000)   # Max neurons to use for SVD initialization\\\\n    projection_layers = []\\\\n    for session in range(nsessions):\\\\n        # Bound once: session_data converts on every subscript, so the four reads\\\\n        # below would otherwise rebuild this session\\\\'s tensors four times.\\\\n        batch = session_data[session]\\\\n        if batch is None:\\\\n            # A session with no trials has no neuron count to size a layer with. Held as\\\\n            # None so that projection_layers stays indexed by session; every read of it is\\\\n            # guarded by the same emptiness check that skips the session\\\\'s data.\\\\n            projection_layers.append(None)\\\\n            continue\\\\n        nneurons = batch[\\\\'nneurons\\\\']\\\\n        proj = nn.Linear(nneurons, npcs, bias=False).to(device)\\\\n\\\\n        # Initialize with SVD for stability (use V from SVD of neural data)\\\\n        # For neural data with shape (n_timepoints, n_neurons), SVD gives principal components in V\\\\n        # Use random subsets if data is too large\\\\n        n_timepoints = batch[\\\\'neural\\\\'].shape[0]\\\\n\\\\n        # Sample timepoints if needed\\\\n        if n_timepoints > svd_max_samples:\\\\n            time_indices = torch.randperm(n_timepoints)[:svd_max_samples]\\\\n            neural_subset = batch[\\\\'neural\\\\'][time_indices, :]\\\\n            print(f\\\"Session {session}: Using {svd_max_samples} of {n_timepoints} timepoints for SVD initialization\\\")\\\\n        else:\\\\n            neural_subset = batch[\\\\'neural\\\\']\\\\n\\\\n        # Use random projection if too many neurons\\\\n        if nneurons > svd_max_neurons:\\\\n            # Create random Gaussian projection matrix: (n_neurons, svd_max_neurons)\\\\n            P = torch.randn(nneurons, svd_max_neurons)\\\\n            # Project neural data: (n_timepoints, n_neurons) @ (n_neurons, svd_max_neurons) = (n_timepoints, svd_max_neurons)\\\\n            neural_projected = neural_subset @ P\\\\n            print(f\\\"Session {session}: Using random projection to {svd_max_neurons} of {nneurons} neurons for SVD initialization\\\")\\\\n\\\\n            # SVD on projected data\\\\n            _, _, V = torch.svd(neural_projected, some=True)\\\\n            # V has shape (svd_max_neurons, min(n_timepoints_subset, svd_max_neurons))\\\\n\\\\n            # Compose: final weight = (P @ V[:, :npcs]).T = V[:, :npcs].T @ P.T\\\\n            n_components = min(npcs, V.shape[1])\\\\n            proj.weight.data[:n_components, :] = (V[:, :n_components].T @ P.T).to(device).clone()\\\\n        else:\\\\n            # Standard SVD without random projection\\\\n            _, _, V = torch.svd(neural_subset, some=True)\\\\n            # V has shape (n_neurons, min(n_timepoints_subset, n_neurons))\\\\n            n_components = min(npcs, V.shape[1])\\\\n            proj.weight.data[:n_components, :] = V[:, :n_components].T.to(device).clone()\\\\n\\\\n        # Initialize remaining components randomly if needed\\\\n        if n_components < npcs:\\\\n            nn.init.orthogonal_(proj.weight.data[n_components:, :])\\\\n\\\\n        projection_layers.append(proj)\\\\n\\\\n    # Initialize separate decoders for each output dimension\\\\n    decoders = []\\\\n    for out_dim in range(doutput):\\\\n        decoder = nn.Linear(npcs + dinput, ncategories[out_dim]).to(device)\\\\n        decoders.append(decoder)\\\\n\\\\n    # Collect all parameters\\\\n    all_params = []\\\\n    for proj in projection_layers:\\\\n        if proj is None:          # session with no trials, nothing to train\\\\n            continue\\\\n        all_params.extend(proj.parameters())\\\\n    for decoder in decoders:\\\\n        all_params.extend(decoder.parameters())\\\\n\\\\n    optimizer = optim.Adam(all_params, lr=lr)\\\\n\\\\n    # Loss function with reduction=\\\\'none\\\\' for manual weighting (matches torch_brain)\\\\n    criterion = nn.CrossEntropyLoss(reduction=\\\\'none\\\\')\\\\n\\\\n    # Training loop\\\\n    # Data stays on CPU; each session is moved to GPU one at a time.\\\\n    # backward() is called per session to free the computation graph before\\\\n    # loading the next session. Gradients accumulate across sessions.\\\\n    for epoch in range(num_epochs):\\\\n        epoch_loss = 0.\\\\n        optimizer.zero_grad()\\\\n\\\\n        # iter_sessions rather than session_data[session]: every session is\\\\n        # converted once per epoch, and converting the next few on other threads\\\\n        # overlaps that with this session\\\\'s forward and backward pass.\\\\n        for session, batch in session_data.iter_sessions():\\\\n            if batch is None:\\\\n                continue\\\\n            # Move this session\\\\'s data to device\\\\n            neural_batch = batch[\\\\'neural\\\\'].to(device)\\\\n            input_batch = batch[\\\\'input\\\\'].to(device)\\\\n            output_batch = batch[\\\\'output\\\\'].to(device)\\\\n\\\\n            # Forward pass - shared projection\\\\n            projected = projection_layers[session](neural_batch)\\\\n            combined = torch.cat([projected, input_batch], dim=1)\\\\n\\\\n            # Compute loss for each output dimension\\\\n            session_loss = torch.tensor(0., device=device)\\\\n            for out_dim in range(doutput):\\\\n                logits = decoders[out_dim](combined)\\\\n                target = output_batch[:, out_dim]\\\\n                loss_per_sample = criterion(logits, target)\\\\n\\\\n                if balanced_loss:\\\\n                    # Manual per-sample weighting (matches torch_brain exactly)\\\\n                    sample_weights = class_weights[out_dim][target]\\\\n                    session_loss += (sample_weights * loss_per_sample).sum() / sample_weights.sum()\\\\n                else:\\\\n                    session_loss += loss_per_sample.mean()\\\\n\\\\n            # Add L1 regularization on current session\\\\'s projection matrix\\\\n            if l1_weight > 0:\\\\n                l1_loss = torch.sum(torch.abs(projection_layers[session].weight))\\\\n                session_loss += l1_weight * l1_loss\\\\n\\\\n            # Backward per session: frees computation graph, accumulates gradients\\\\n            session_loss.backward()\\\\n            epoch_loss += session_loss.item()\\\\n\\\\n        optimizer.step()\\\\n\\\\n        if ((epoch + 1) % 10 == 0) or (epoch < 10):\\\\n            # Loss is sum of per-output-dim means across sessions\\\\n            # Normalize by (nsessions * doutput) to get average per output per session\\\\n            train_loss_normalized = epoch_loss / (nsessions * doutput)\\\\n            print(f\\\\'Epoch [{epoch+1}/{num_epochs}], Loss: {train_loss_normalized:.6f}\\\\')\\\\n\\\\n    # Store trained parameters. A session with no trials contributes None, keeping\\\\n    # model[\\\\'projection\\\\'] indexed by session for predict(), which reads it only for\\\\n    # sessions that have trials.\\\\n    for session in range(nsessions):\\\\n        proj = projection_layers[session]\\\\n        model[\\\\'projection\\\\'].append(None if proj is None else proj.weight.data.clone())\\\\n    model[\\\\'decoder\\\\'] = decoders\\\\n\\\\n    return model\\\\n\\\\ndef predict(neural: list, input: list, model: dict, sessionid: list | None = None, metadata: dict = {}):\\\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\\\n    Uses a trained decoder model to predict output from neural and input data.\\\\n\\\\n    Inputs:\\\\n        neural: neural activity. neural is a list of length nsessions, neural[session] is a list\\\\n                of length ntrials[session], and neural[session][trial] is a numpy array of shape (nneurons[session], T[session][trial]),\\\\n                dtype = float32, giving the neural activity for that trial\\\\n        input: variables beyond neural activity the decoder should input, e.g. the stimulis, condition. input is a list of\\\\n                length nsessions, input[session] is a list of length ntrials[session], and input[session][trial] is a numpy array of\\\\n                shape (dinput, T[session][trial]), dtype = float32. with the input variable(s) for each timepoint.\\\\n                dinput is the number of inputs. For discrete inputs, use a one-hot encoding.\\\\n    return scores, all_predictions, all_pcs, all_confidences, trial_splits\\\\n\\\\n\\\\ndef train_validate_decoder(neural: list, input: list, output: list, metadata: str | None = None,\\\\n                            frac_train: float = DEFAULT_FRAC_TRAIN,\\\\n                            eval_fn=None, state=None,\\\\n                            **kwargs):\\\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\\\n    Validates the decoder by training on frac_train of trials and testing on the rest.\\\\n    Every session has some trials in training and some in test.\\\\n\\\\n    Inputs:\\\\n        neural: neural activity. neural is a list of length nsessions, neural[session] is a list\\\\n                of length ntrials[session], and neural[session][trial] is a numpy array of shape (nneurons[session], T[session][trial]),\\\\n                dtype = float32, giving the neural activity for that trial\\\\n        input: variables beyond neural activity the decoder should input, e.g. the stimulis, condition. input is a list of\\\\n                length nsessions, input[session] is a list of length ntrials[session], and input[session][trial] is a numpy array of\\\\n                shape (dinput, T[session][trial]), dtype = float32. with the input variable(s) for each timepoint.\\\\n                dinput is the number of inputs. Should have the signature\\\\n                scores = eval_fn(predictions, output)\\\\n                Default: accuracy_all_sessions\\\\n        state: optional dict with random state for reproducibility. Should contain \\\\'numpy\\\\' and \\\\'torch\\\\' keys with the random seeds\\\\n                Default: None (random state will be generated and returned in output)\\\\n\\\\n    kwargs are passed to train_decoder\\\\n\\\\n    Returns:\\\\n        scores: numpy array of shape (doutput,) with the evaluation metric for each output dimension\\\\n        predictions: list of length nsessions, predictions[session] is a list of length ntrials[session],\\\\n                and predictions[session][trial] is a numpy array of shape (doutput, T[session][trial])\\\\n                with the predicted output variable(s) for each timepoint. Work in float32 to keep the full conversion tractable.\\\\n   105\\\\t    corrected = f - NEUROPIL_COEF * f_neu\\\\n   106\\\\t    corrected += NEUROPIL_COEF * np.mean(f_neu, axis=1, keepdims=True)\\\\n   107\\\\t\\\\n   108\\\\t    baseline = gaussian_filter1d(corrected, 15, axis=1)\\\\n   109\\\\t    baseline = minimum_filter1d(baseline, BASELINE_WINDOW_SAMPLES, axis=1)\\\\n   110\\\\t    baseline = maximum_filter1d(baseline, BASELINE_WINDOW_SAMPLES, axis=1)\\\\n   111\\\\t    with np.errstate(divide=\\\"ignore\\\", invalid=\\\"ignore\\\"):\\\\n   112\\\\t        dff = (corrected - baseline) / np.abs(baseline)\\\\n   113\\\\t    dff = gaussian_filter1d(dff, 2, axis=1)\\\\n   114\\\\t    if not np.isfinite(dff).all():\\\\n   115\\\\t        # Degenerate zero baselines are not expected in curated ROIs. Preserve the\\\\n   116\\\\t        # usable samples without allowing NaNs/Infs into the decoder.\\\\n   117\\\\t        dff = np.nan_to_num(dff, copy=False)\\\\n   118\\\\t    events = substantially\\\\n   139\\\\t        # faster in h5py than a large two-dimensional fancy selection.\\\\n   140\\\\t        slab = np.asarray(series[plane_name][\\\"data\\\"][start:stop, :], dtype=np.float32)\\\\n   141\\\\t        pieces.append(slab[:, local_keep])\\\\n   142\\\\t        global_indices.append(plane_global[local_keep])\\\\n   143\\\\t\\\\n   144\\\\t    values = np.concatenate(pieces, axis=1)\\\\n   145\\\\t    order = np.argsort(np.concatenate(global_indices))\\\\n   190\\\\t 196\\\\t        outcomes = np.zeros(len(starts), dtype=np.int8)\\\\n   197\\\\t        for trial, (start, stop) in enumerate(zip(starts, stops)):\\\\n   198\\\\t            left = np.sum(dff64, axis=1)\\\\n   232\\\\t            sx2 += np.sum(dff64 * dff64, axis=1)\\\\n   233\\\\t            sxy += dff64 @ trial_speed\\\\n   234\\\\t            sy += Assigning reward zone location for each trial: \n- Before converting, the `survey()` function was used to try to understand all the data values, including whatever is going on with the `reward_zone` variable. It first computes the position of the min and max position of the mouse when `reward_zone` is non-zero:\n```python\nbehavior_data = nwbfile.processing['behavior']['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series       \ninfo['reward_zone_positions'] = np.full((ntrials, 2), np.nan)\nposition = behavior_data['position'].data[:]\nfor trial in range(ntrials):\n    idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial], trial_end_idx[trial], dtype=int)\n    pos_trial = position[idx]\n    reward_zone_trial = reward_zone[idx] > 0\n    if not np.any(reward_zone_trial):\n        continue\n    info['reward_zone_positions'][trial] = [np.min(pos_trial[reward_zone_trial]), np.max(pos_trial[reward_zone_trial])]\n```\nThe function `segment_reward_positions()` uses the Viterbi algorithm to assign reward zone labels so that the initial rewarded positions are close to the corresponding rewarded zone:\n```python\nreward_zone_dict = {'A': [80, 130],\n                    'B': [200, 250],\n                    'C': [320, 370]}\n...\nsigma = 10.0  # std dev for Gaussian emission on zone distance\nzone_ranges = np.array([reward_zone_dict[z] for z in zone_names])  # (n_states, 2)\ndist = np.maximum(zone_ranges[None, :, 0] - startpoints[valid, None],\n                    startpoints[valid, None] - zone_ranges[None, :, 1]) # negative if inside the zone, positive if outside\ndist = np.maximum(dist, 0)\nlog_emission[valid] = -0.5 * (dist / sigma) ** 2\n```\nIt also encourages the reward zone label to not change often:\n```python\npstay = .9\nlog_trans = np.full((n_states, n_states), np.log((1 - pstay) / (n_states - 1)))\nnp.fill_diagonal(log_trans, np.log(pstay))\n```\n\niii. The paper defines reward zones A, B, C at specific position ranges. These same ranges could also be found, with some translation, in the code (required mapping X -> A, etc.). I plotted the position of the mouse when `reward_zone` was positive (note that the value was between 0 and 6 I think) to determine that the Viterbi approach would likely work, and that the start of the rewarded position was the least noisy variable to use. The class fractions were approximately 1/3 after this, so it seemed correct. \n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Distance to reward zone*?\n\ni. For each timepoint, compute the signed distance from the animal's position to the nearest edge of the reward zone. Distance is 0 when inside the zone, negative when before the zone, and positive when past it.  The continuous distance is then discretized into 7 bins using `np.digitize` with bin edges `[-inf, -50, -10, 0, 1e-6, 10, 50, inf]`.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef compute_distance_to_reward_zone(position_curr, reward_zone_curr):\n    ntimepoints = len(position_curr)\n    reward_zone_range = reward_zone_dict[reward_zone_curr]\n    distance_to_reward_zone = np.zeros(ntimepoints)\n    idx_before_zone = position_curr < reward_zone_range[0]\n    distance_to_reward_zone[idx_before_zone] = position_curr[idx_before_zone] - reward_zone_range[0]\n    idx_after_zone = position_curr > reward_zone_range[1]\n    distance_to_reward_zone[idx_after_zone] = position_curr[idx_after_zone] - reward_zone_range[1]\n    return distance_to_reward_zone\n\n```\n```python\ndistance_to_reward_zone_bins = [-np.inf, -50, -10, 0, 1e-6, 10, 50, np.inf]\n...\noutput_curr[0] = np.digitize(distance_to_reward_zone, distance_to_reward_zone_bins) - 1\n```\n\n\niii. This matches the paper's concept of distance relative to the reward zone. The reward zone boundaries are from `reward_zone_dict`, inspired by the reference code `Sosa_et_al_2024/src/reward_relative/behavior.py`. The `1e-6` boundary separates exactly 0 (in the zone boundary) from slightly positive. The `-1` corrects `np.digitize` to be 0-indexed.\n\n## 7-c. How is `output` *Distance to reward zone* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. The continuous distance is discretized into 7 bins using `np.digitize` with bin edges `[-inf, -50, -10, 0, 1e-6, 10, 50, inf]`.\n\nii.\n```python\ndistance_to_reward_zone_bins = [-np.inf, -50, -10, 0, 1e-6, 10, 50, np.inf]\n...\noutput_curr[0] = np.digitize(distance_to_reward_zone, distance_to_reward_zone_bins) - 1\n```\n\niii. The bin edges match the instructions. The `1e-6` boundary separates exactly 0 (in the zone boundary) from slightly positive. The `-1` corrects `np.digitize` to be 0-indexed.\n\n## 7-d. How is `output` *Distance to reward zone* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The position data and neural data share the same time indices within each trial, so no additional alignment is needed.\n\nii.\n```python\nidx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial], trial_end_idx[trial], dtype=int)\nspike_data_curr = spike_data[idx]\nposition_curr = position[idx]\n```\n\niii. Both neural and behavioral data use the same time indexing (verified by timestamp assertions earlier in the code), so indexing by the same `idx` array ensures alignment.\n\n## 8-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Absolute position* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `position` behavior time series.\n\nii.\n```python\nposition = behavior_data['position'].data[:][:T]\nassert np.allclose(behavior_data['position'].timestamps[:][:T], timestamps), \"Timestamps for position do not match trial number timestamps\"\n...\nposition_curr = position[idx]\n```\n\niii. The `position` variable directly records the animal's position in the VR corridor in cm.\n\n## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Absolute position*?\n\ni. No processing beyond extracting the per-trial slice and discretizing. Discretized into 5 bins using `np.digitize` with bin edges `[-inf, 90, 180, 270, 360, inf]`, corresponding to 90 cm wide bins spanning the 450 cm track. Within the converted trials the position ranges from -0.8 to 451.8 cm, so the open first and last bins absorb the few samples that fall just outside the track (185 of 1,307,665). \n\nii.\n```python\nposition_curr = position[idx]\noutput_curr[1] = np.digitize(position_curr, position_bins) - 1\n```\n\n```python\nposition_bins = [-np.inf,90,180,270,360,np.inf]\n...\noutput_curr[1] = np.digitize(position_curr, position_bins) - 1\n```\n\n\niii. The raw position values are used directly. Each lap starts at 0 cm and the track is 450 cm long (Methods), so five 90 cm bins over 0 to 450 are equal-sized by construction. The survey's wider -50 to 450 range comes from including the inter-trial teleport period, which is not part of any converted trial.\n\n## 8-c. How is `output` *Absolute position* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Discretized into 5 bins using `np.digitize` with bin edges `[-inf, 90, 180, 270, 360, inf]`, corresponding to 90 cm wide bins spanning the 450 cm track. \n\nii.\n```python\nposition_bins = [-np.inf,90,180,270,360,np.inf]\n...\noutput_curr[1] = np.digitize(position_curr, position_bins) - 1\n```\n\niii. The instructions specify \"5 equal-sized bins\" spanning the 450 cm track, which is 90 cm per bin. The first and last edges are left open so that samples marginally outside the track fall in the end bins rather than in a class of their own.\n\n## 8-d. How is `output` *Absolute position* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Same time indices as the neural data within each trial — no additional alignment needed.\n\nii. Same indexing as neural: `position_curr = position[idx]`\n\niii. Verified by timestamp assertions.\n\n## 9-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Lick* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `lick` behavior time series.\n\nii.\n```python\nlicks = behavior_data['lick'].data[:][:T]\nassert np.allclose(behavior_data['lick'].timestamps[:][:T], timestamps), \"Timestamps for lick do not match trial number timestamps\"\n...\nlicks_curr = licks[idx]\n```\n\niii. The `lick` variable records lick events at each timepoint.\n\n## 9-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Lick*?\n\ni. Binarized: any positive lick value is mapped to 1, otherwise 0.\n\nii.\n```python\noutput_curr[3] = (licks_curr > 0).astype(int)\n```\n\niii. The instructions specify binary output (no/yes). The raw lick values can be >1, so thresholding at >0 converts to binary.\n\n## 9-c. How is `output` *Lick* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The `Reward` time series records reward delivery events with separate timestamps from the behavior sampling rate.\n\n## 11-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Reward outcome*?\n\ni. Reward event timestamps are mapped to the nearest behavior timepoint using `searchsorted`. For each trial, the output is 1 if any reward event occurred within the trial, 0 otherwise. The value is constant across all timepoints in the trial.\n\nii.\n```python\nreward_time_indices = np.searchsorted(timestamps, reward_times)\nerr = timestamps[reward_time_indices] - reward_times\nassert np.all(np.abs(err) <= time_bin_size/2)\nisreward = np.zeros(T)\nisreward[reward_time_indices] = 1\n...\noutput_curr[5] = np.any(isreward[idx]).astype(int)\n```\n\niii. The alignment error is checked to be within half a time bin. `np.any` is used because a trial either was rewarded or not — the specific timepoint doesn't matter for the per-trial binary output.\n\n## 12. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Several cases are handled:\n- **Neural/behavior length mismatch**: If the number of neural time bins differs from behavior timestamps, data is cropped to the minimum of the two with a warning printed.\n- **Short trials**: Trials with fewer than 50 timepoints (`min_ntimepoints`) are skipped with a warning.\n- **Missing reward zone data**: Trials where `reward_zone` is never active (all zeros) get NaN positions, and the Viterbi algorithm handles these via uniform emission (assignment driven by neighboring trials' transitions).\n- **Reward timestamp alignment**: An assertion verifies reward times align within half a time bin of behavior timestamps.\n\nii.\n```python\nif T != len(timestamps):\n    print(f\"Warning: number of neural time bins ({T}) does not match number of behavior timestamps ({len(timestamps)}), cropping to the minimum of the two\")\n    T = min(T, len(timestamps))\n    spike_data = spike_data[:T]\n    timestamps = timestamps[:T]\n...\nif idx.sum() < min_ntimepoints:\n    print(f\"Skipping trial {trial}...\")\n    istrial[trial] = False\n    continue\n```\n\niii. These are defensive checks found during data exploration. The neural/behavior mismatch occurs in a small number of sessions.\n\n## 13-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. The most time-consuming steps are:\n1. **Loading NWB files** (`pynwb.NWBHDF5IO` and reading large arrays) — I/O bound\n2. **The survey step** — loads every NWB file to gather statistics and reward zone positions\n3. **Full conversion** — loads every NWB file again and extracts all trial data\n4. **Saving the .pkl file**\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. The NWB files are large (contain full neural recordings). Conversion is otherwise quite fast\n\n## 13-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. Some operations like `np.digitize` and `compute_distance_to_reward_zone` could theoretically be applied to the full session arrays before splitting into trials, but the variable trial lengths make this awkward. The Viterbi inner loop (lines 503-507) iterates over states, which could be vectorized with matrix operations.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii.
